## Full Library — Compassion Only (No Preferences)

This section contains **all 281 accepted works** and does **not** use verified-title preferences, personal vibe, popularity, watch status, or user ratings.

**Neutral score formula:** `(25 centrality + 20 consequences + 15 intensity + 10 frequency + 10 empathy + 10 helping + 5 max(redemption, found family, moral courage) + 5 emotional depth) / 5`

<a id="detailed-audits"></a>
## Detailed Audits

<a id="work-alien-nation"></a>
### Alien Nation

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 89.9  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 89.1  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0096531/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alien_Nation_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

Human and alien partners work in a society absorbing extraterrestrial refugees.

#### Plot overview

Human and alien partners work in a society absorbing extraterrestrial refugees. The story especially follows Matthew Sikes, who moves from prejudiced human detective toward genuine partner, friend and advocate as ordinary contact dismantles species abstractions. Alongside George Francisco, the plot develops through balances gratitude for refuge with insistence that Newcomers deserve rights rather than conditional tolerance; family and police work make him a civic as well as personal compassion figure.

#### Compassion analysis

Alien Nation uses police procedural and family drama to make prejudice against the Tenctonese/Newcomers an everyday moral problem rather than a single allegorical episode. Newcomers arrive as former slaves and are immediately treated as an alien population to segregate, fetishize, fear or exploit. The Francisco family and Sikes partnership steadily make abstract species prejudice impossible to maintain because work, parenting, friendship, sexuality, religion and civic participation all require recognizing Newcomers as neighbors and citizens.

**Why it matches:** It is an excellent serious match to Sanctuary, Humans and Enemy Mine: compassion is cross-species, anti-collective-guilt and structural. Unlike some speculative series, it repeatedly moves beyond individual friendship into voting rights, policing, discrimination, media representation and family integration.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~93 events · ~325 compassion-focused minutes · ~32% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The Newcomers arrive as survivors of slavery** — The opening narration repeatedly reminds viewers that the alien population did not invade; they were captive laborers stranded on Earth.
- **Sikes must unlearn species prejudice through partnership** — His relationship with George turns slurs and assumptions into increasingly intimate knowledge of Tenctonese family, biology and culture.
- **George's dignity is ordinary rather than exceptional** — He is not valuable because he proves aliens are superhumanly virtuous; he is a cop, husband, father and sometimes flawed person entitled to equal standing.
- **The Francisco family makes immigration/refugee questions domestic** — Children, pregnancy, schooling, neighbors and generational tension keep the political allegory rooted in ordinary care.
- **Species prejudice includes fetishization as well as hatred** — Newcomers are sexualized, exoticized or treated as curiosities, showing that apparent fascination can still deny personhood.
- **Newcomer children face inherited discrimination** — Buck and Emily encounter hostility and pressure around assimilation while still developing identities connected to Tenctonese traditions.
- **Voting rights become an explicit structural compassion issue** — The Spirit of '95 centers the Alien Vote Amendment and treats civic voice as part of full personhood rather than optional generosity from humans.
- **Purist violence is collective-guilt ideology** — The anti-Newcomer movement argues an entire species threatens human destiny, translating fear into segregation, bombing and intimidation.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The allegory is sometimes very direct** — Newcomer prejudice maps onto race, immigration and civil-rights histories in ways that can flatten important differences among real-world experiences.
- **Police institutions receive more optimism than scrutiny** — The series critiques biased individuals and policies while often assuming good officers can repair systemic problems from within.
- **Some alien biology is played for exotic comedy** — Cultural curiosity can occasionally reproduce the same othering the series otherwise criticizes.
- **One season limits long-term structural development** — The show raises citizenship and integration questions larger than its original run can fully resolve.

#### Representative evidence

- **Pilot / TV Movie** — Sikes is paired with George despite open human prejudice.
- **1x04 Little Lost Lamb** — The episode opens by explicitly describing Newcomers as former slaves stranded on Earth.
- **1x13 The Red Room** — Trauma and the Newcomers' past as engineered slaves become relevant to present crimes and behavior.
- **1x14 The Spirit of '95** — Newcomers campaign for voting rights while a Purist movement uses bomb threats and species-purity rhetoric.
- **1x14 family/political scenes** — Susan and Buck continue canvassing despite threats because civic voice matters to the family.
- **1x15 Generation to Generation** — Family rituals and intergenerational Newcomer life are treated as ordinary domestic reality, not merely alien custom.

<a id="work-avatar-the-last-airbender"></a>
### Avatar: The Last Airbender

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 87.5  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 87.1  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0417299/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avatar:_The_Last_Airbender)

#### Synopsis

Avatar places compassion inside a war created by imperialism and genocide. Its moral achievement is not pretending everyone is harmless; it repeatedly separates people from nations, ancestry from destiny, anger from necessary cruelty, and justice from revenge. Aang's refusal to kill Ozai is the most famous expression, but the same ethic appears throughout smaller encounters.

#### Plot overview

The story especially follows Aang, who carries a culture of nonviolence into a war demanding decisive action. His growth is learning that mercy must coexist with responsibility, leadership and the power to stop harm. Alongside Zuko, the plot develops through one of the strongest redemption arcs in the library: abuse and indoctrination explain his obsession, but only accountability, changed loyalties and repair allow trust.

#### Compassion analysis

Avatar places compassion inside a war created by imperialism and genocide. Its moral achievement is not pretending everyone is harmless; it repeatedly separates people from nations, ancestry from destiny, anger from necessary cruelty, and justice from revenge. Aang's refusal to kill Ozai is the most famous expression, but the same ethic appears throughout smaller encounters.

**Why it matches:** It closely matches the verified set through serious compassion under danger, personhood over enemy category, rehabilitation with accountability, found family, rejection of inherited guilt, and the belief that a person raised inside a violent system can make different choices.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~115 events · ~379 compassion-focused minutes · ~27% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Enemy nationality is not moral identity** — The series repeatedly humanizes Fire Nation civilians, children, soldiers and dissidents while remaining unequivocal about imperial aggression. Opposition to a regime is separated from hatred of a people.
- **Zuko's redemption is earned, not granted by sympathy alone** — His suffering explains his behavior but does not entitle him to trust. Joining the group requires confession, changed action, risk, patience with others' anger, and repeated proof.
- **Aang's pacifism is tested rather than protected from difficulty** — The finale forces him to face an enemy whose continued power threatens mass death. His search for a nonlethal solution is moral problem-solving under extreme pressure, not simple refusal to act.
- **Mercy does not mean leaving a perpetrator powerful** — Ozai is spared but stripped of the power enabling further atrocities. Nonlethal restraint is presented as compatible with decisive protection.
- **Katara distinguishes mercy from forgiveness** — In The Southern Raiders, she refuses to kill the man responsible for her mother's death but does not forgive him. Compassion constrains revenge without requiring the victim to absolve the perpetrator.
- **Aang validates anger while arguing against becoming it** — He does not tell Katara her grief is illegitimate. His challenge concerns what anger should be allowed to make her do.
- **The Blue Spirit humanizes Zuko before redemption** — Aang saves the masked rescuer after discovering he is Zuko and later wonders whether another history might have allowed friendship. Compassion appears before evidence Zuko will change.
- **Fire itself is morally reinterpreted** — The dragons teach Aang and Zuko that fire is life and energy, not merely rage or destruction. Understanding repairs both a feared power and the people who wield it.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Energybending arrives late** — Aang's nonlethal solution depends on a new spiritual technique introduced near the finale, reducing how fully the story works through the practical cost of his ethic.
- **Some enemy soldiers remain faceless** — Large battles necessarily turn many Fire Nation combatants into obstacles even while the broader series argues against categorical dehumanization.
- **Forgiveness can be oversimplified by viewers** — The Southern Raiders specifically avoids forced forgiveness, but broader spiritual language can be misread as requiring victims to release anger on demand.
- **The heroes also use coercion and violence** — Compassion is a governing ethic, not a claim that the protagonists are consistently nonviolent or autonomy-perfect.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x13 The Blue Spirit** — Zuko rescues Aang for self-interested reasons; Aang still saves Zuko when escape would be easier alone.
- **2x07 Zuko Alone** — Zuko's family history and ordinary Earth Kingdom lives complicate his self-image and the audience's view of him.
- **2x16 Appa's Lost Days** — Appa's suffering is treated as a serious trauma narrative rather than an animal-comedy subplot.
- **3x12 The Western Air Temple** — Zuko asks to join people he previously hunted.
- **3x13 The Firebending Masters** — Aang and Zuko discover fire as life and energy rather than destruction.
- **3x16 The Southern Raiders** — Katara confronts her mother's killer.

<a id="work-batteries-not-included"></a>
### Batteries Not Included

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 93.3  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 90.9  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0092494/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batteries_Not_Included)

#### Synopsis

Residents facing eviction encounter tiny alien machines.

#### Plot overview

Residents facing eviction encounter tiny alien machines. The story especially follows Frank Riley, who a proud, exhausted building owner and husband learns to accept help from neighbors and nonhuman visitors rather than carrying resistance and caregiving alone. Alongside Faye Riley, the plot develops through her dementia makes her vulnerable to disorientation and loss, but the film repeatedly preserves her emotional personhood and place in the community.

#### Compassion analysis

Batteries Not Included is a community-care story disguised as a gentle alien fantasy. A small group of elderly, poor and socially marginalized tenants are treated as obstacles by a developer and gradually become a mutually protective household. The tiny mechanical aliens do not simply rescue them; care becomes reciprocal as the residents shelter, repair and protect the aliens, while the aliens repair both machines and a damaged human community.

**Why it matches:** It strongly matches Good Witch's practical, everyday care and The Wild Robot's nonhuman reciprocity. Compassion is not abstract: residents feed one another, protect Faye as her dementia worsens, accept Harry's muteness and isolation, shelter the alien machines, and resist a system that treats vulnerable people as disposable.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~13 events · ~46 compassion-focused minutes · ~43% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The tenants are treated as disposable by redevelopment logic** — The eviction campaign assumes elderly and low-income residents can simply be removed once their building becomes economically inconvenient.
- **Frank's resistance is community protection, not only stubbornness** — He keeps the café and building functioning because they are the social center holding the remaining residents together.
- **Faye's dementia is met with imperfect but persistent care** — Her confusion creates danger and strain, yet the household continues searching for, feeding and protecting her rather than treating her as a burden to discard.
- **Harry is included despite near-total withdrawal** — His muteness and isolation do not remove him from the moral community; others watch for him, feed him and eventually recognize his competence and loyalty.
- **Marisa receives practical support rather than judgment** — Her pregnancy and economic vulnerability become part of the group's collective concern rather than a reason for exclusion.
- **The aliens are initially cared for before their usefulness is known** — Residents offer shelter and repair to the tiny machines before fully understanding what they can do in return.
- **Care becomes reciprocal rather than charitable** — The mechanical visitors repair appliances, the building and eventually devastated homes, turning hospitality into mutual aid rather than one-way rescue.
- **The alien family is recognized as family** — When one of the small machines is damaged or the infants are threatened, the human tenants respond emotionally and protectively rather than as owners of useful technology.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Dementia is sometimes used for comedy and plot convenience** — Faye's confusion receives warmth, but the film occasionally softens the seriousness of cognitive decline.
- **The developer side is morally simplified** — Corporate displacement is embodied by conspicuously cruel agents, leaving limited exploration of the broader structural forces behind gentrification.
- **Alien intervention makes material repair unusually easy** — The final physical restoration is magical in a way real displaced communities cannot access.
- **Caregiving burdens fall heavily on a small group** — The film values community, but does not deeply examine what sustained support for Faye and other vulnerable residents would require.

#### Representative evidence

- **Early eviction pressure** — Residents are threatened and harassed because they refuse to leave.
- **Searching for Faye** — When Faye wanders or becomes confused, others organize around locating and protecting her rather than blaming her.
- **First contact with the Fix-Its** — The residents respond to the tiny machines with wonder and shelter rather than immediate exploitation.
- **Alien family/offspring** — The humans respond protectively to the machines' young and to damaged members of the alien family.
- **Harry's participation** — A man everyone tends to overlook becomes crucial in defending the community and caring for the small aliens.
- **Carlos's moral hesitation** — The escalating intimidation becomes too cruel even for someone hired to force the residents out.

<a id="work-being-human-uk"></a>
### Being Human (UK)

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 90.4  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 84.1  
**Vibe class:** TONAL OUTLIER  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt1349938/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Being_Human_(British_TV_series))

#### Synopsis

A vampire, werewolf and ghost attempt an ordinary shared life.

#### Plot overview

A vampire, werewolf and ghost attempt an ordinary shared life. The story especially follows Mitchell, who attempts to build a humane life, relapses catastrophically, hides harm, seeks forgiveness and finally accepts that love does not give him the right to endanger future victims. Alongside George, the plot develops through begins horrified by being a werewolf and dependent on Mitchell; grows into partner, father and finally the person forced to put ethics above preserving his best friend.

#### Compassion analysis

Being Human is one of the library's most severe tests of the idea that monster identity is not destiny. Vampires, werewolves and ghosts try to build ordinary domestic life while living with predatory impulses, bodily danger, death and addiction-like relapse. Compassion is not presented as magic cure: loving someone can help them resist, but love can also enable harm, and accountability sometimes requires separation or even lethal intervention.

**Why it matches:** It is exceptionally close to Charmed and Legacies in category—supernatural people are not evil by essence—but darker in consequence. Mitchell's arc especially asks whether understanding and love can coexist with the fact that someone has committed unforgivable harm. Hal later revisits the same problem through long-term relapse prevention.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~164 events · ~689 compassion-focused minutes · ~33% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The household is an experiment in chosen humanity** — A vampire, werewolf and ghost build ordinary routines because domestic relationship gives them a structure for becoming the people they want to be.
- **Supernatural condition is not moral essence** — The series repeatedly distinguishes being a vampire/werewolf/ghost from the choices made within that condition.
- **Addiction is a major compassion framework** — Blood hunger and vampiric relapse are treated partly like addiction: support helps, but relapse remains possible and can catastrophically harm others.
- **Mitchell's love does not erase the Box Tunnel massacre** — The show forces his friends to confront that the person they love committed mass murder; sympathy cannot replace justice.
- **George's final decision about Mitchell is compassion plus accountability** — Mitchell asks to be permanently stopped because he believes he will kill again. George's action is framed as love, horror and responsibility simultaneously.
- **Victims retain moral priority** — Mitchell explicitly recognizes that forgiveness belongs to victims, not friends who were not harmed, rejecting easy proxy absolution.
- **Annie's compassion repeatedly crosses ontological boundaries** — As a ghost, she remains emotionally invested in living people and supernatural friends rather than withdrawing into death.
- **Revenge can reproduce the harm it claims to answer** — Lia's revenge plot eventually confronts the fact that pain has not actually been repaired by manipulating others toward suffering.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Redemption can reach lethal limits** — The series sometimes concludes that a loved person cannot safely be allowed another chance, distinguishing compassion from endless rescue.
- **Friends can enable horrific behavior** — Love leads characters to hide crimes or delay accountability, and the show treats this as corruption rather than pure loyalty.
- **Some supernatural enemies remain categorical threats** — Not every vampire or supernatural antagonist receives the interiority given to main characters.
- **Violent death and trauma are frequent** — Compassion often operates after severe irreversible harm rather than preventing it.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x01** — The central household is already organized around helping three supernatural people live ordinary, non-predatory lives.
- **2x07** — George and Nina pursue a supposed cure because they believe they have responsibility to reduce the danger they pose.
- **3x04 The Pack** — Tom's father frames his own revenge-driven upbringing as something Tom should eventually escape.
- **3x08 The Wolf-Shaped Bullet** — Annie refuses to free Mitchell merely because she loves him after learning the scale of his murders.
- **series-wide Mitchell recovery/relapse arc** — Friends provide structure that genuinely helps Mitchell live differently for periods of time.
- **series 4 Hal/Tom/Alex household** — A new supernatural household recreates the chosen-family experiment with different histories.

<a id="work-buffy-the-vampire-slayer"></a>
### Buffy the Vampire Slayer

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 80.6  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 82.4  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0118276/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffy_the_Vampire_Slayer)

#### Synopsis

A young slayer protects a town while relying on friends.

#### Plot overview

A young slayer protects a town while relying on friends. The story especially follows Buffy Summers, who moves from lonely chosen weapon toward leader of a chosen family and finally toward sharing power. Her best compassion lies in protecting people others classify as burdens while learning not to define herself only by sacrifice. Alongside Spike, the plot develops through moves from predatory antagonist through selfish attachment, catastrophic violation, deliberate pursuit of a soul, and eventually costly service. His arc is useful precisely because it tests the limits of compassion and accountability.

#### Compassion analysis

Buffy is more compassion-centered than its monster-slaying premise suggests, but unevenly so. Its strongest ethic is that moral worth is not reliably determined by species, supernatural condition, past identity or blood family. The Scoobies repeatedly become chosen family for people rejected by biological relatives; several major 'monsters' become friends, lovers or allies; and redemption is possible but usually tied to accountability, suffering and changed behavior.

**Why it matches:** Its closest overlaps with Charmed and Legacies are found family, supernatural identity not equaling evil, rehabilitation after severe wrongdoing, and compassion persisting under danger. It is harsher and more inconsistent than those anchors: many demons remain disposable threats, and the show sometimes relies on soul/no-soul metaphysics to simplify responsibility.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~484 events · ~1548 compassion-focused minutes · ~25% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Chosen family can override abusive biological family** — Family makes the principle explicit when Tara's relatives claim ownership over her and the Scoobies collectively identify themselves as her family.
- **Demon identity is not a sufficient moral verdict** — Anya, Spike, Clem, Angel and others demonstrate that supernatural category does not map cleanly onto good/evil, even though the series applies this inconsistently.
- **Faith is offered a path back after serious harm** — Buffy initially tries to reach Faith after the accidental killing and later accepts that punishment, remorse and changed action can coexist with eventual reintegration.
- **Spike's redemption is difficult rather than sentimental** — His capacity for attachment appears before a soul, but coercion and violence remain real. Seeking a soul after a catastrophic violation marks the beginning of a more accountable form of change, not retroactive innocence.
- **Angel's curse separates condition from aspiration** — The series repeatedly asks whether a vampire with a restored conscience can choose a moral life despite predatory nature and a murderous history.
- **Grief is given full moral weight** — The Body strips away supernatural metaphor and lets shock, practical care, silence and helplessness occupy the episode rather than turning death into a lesson quickly.
- **Buffy's protection of Dawn becomes family by choice** — Dawn's constructed origin does not reduce her personhood. Buffy chooses to treat their lived relationship as real even when biology and memory are revealed as artificial.
- **Sacrifice is meaningful but martyrdom is questioned** — Buffy's death for Dawn is loving, yet later seasons show the damage caused when others assume Buffy can carry every burden indefinitely.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Many demons remain disposable** — The anti-essentialist ethic is strongest for recurring characters; numerous episodic demons are killed without comparable interest in motive or personhood.
- **Soul metaphysics can simplify accountability** — The soul/no-soul distinction sometimes lets the narrative separate moral selves too cleanly, especially around Angel and Spike.
- **Some abusive behavior receives uneven narrative consequences** — Major characters can receive more contextual understanding and forgiveness than one-off antagonists or victims receive.
- **Protective relationships can become controlling** — Buffy, Willow, Giles and others sometimes override autonomy in the name of safety, and the show varies in how strongly it critiques that.

#### Representative evidence

- **3x14 Bad Girls** — Buffy tries to make Faith face an accidental killing rather than suppress it.
- **5x06 Family** — Tara's relatives claim blood-family authority and use a false demon story to control women.
- **5x16 The Body** — Joyce's death is treated without supernatural rescue.
- **5x18 Intervention** — Buffy's fear that she is losing the ability to love is answered through relationships rather than mystical purity.
- **6x22 Grave** — Willow is beyond ordinary force after Tara's death and accumulated rage.
- **7x17 Lies My Parents Told Me** — Spike's trauma and violent conditioning are treated as relevant to present behavior.

<a id="work-charmed-2018"></a>
### Charmed (2018)

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 84.1  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 87.1  
**Vibe class:** VERIFIED ANCHOR  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt6394324/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charmed_(2018_TV_series))

#### Synopsis

Three sisters discover they are the Charmed Ones and inherit a dangerous responsibility to protect innocents and the wider magical world while building a family they did not begin with.

#### Plot overview

Three sisters discover they are the Charmed Ones and inherit a dangerous responsibility to protect innocents and the wider magical world while building a family they did not begin with.

#### Compassion analysis

Compassion is structurally central rather than decorative. The formal mission is protecting innocents, but the series repeatedly asks who counts as innocent, whether dangerous people can be saved instead of destroyed, whether species or ancestry determines moral worth, whether institutions may erase autonomy in the name of safety, and what obligations power creates toward the wider magical community.

**Why it matches:** 

**Occurrence estimate:** ~248 events · ~743 compassion-focused minutes · ~24% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Protection as an obligation created by power** — The Charmed Ones' power creates a duty to protect rather than merely defeat. Protection repeatedly includes people caught inside threats, beings already labeled dangerous, and magical groups outside the sisters' immediate circle.
- **Saving the person inside the threat** — The show rejects the easy assumption that possession, corruption, or dangerous supernatural status makes a person disposable. Angela Wu is an early model: the sisters pursue an unsanctioned exorcism because her soul is still present.
- **Personhood over supernatural category** — Demon, witch, monster, human, chaotic being, and magical creature are repeatedly shown to be inadequate moral categories. Parker and Abigael are especially important because the show allows demon identity to coexist with decency, trauma, conflict, and capacity to change.
- **Mercy without erasing harm** — Compassion does not require pretending past harm never happened. The show instead asks whether punishment reproduces injustice, whether descendants should inherit guilt, and whether someone who has harmed others can still be allowed to atone.
- **Rejection of inherited and collective guilt** — Jordan is threatened because of an ancestor's atrocity. The original harm is acknowledged, but the episode rejects punishing him for a crime he did not commit.
- **Redemption is possible but not automatic** — The show repeatedly permits morally compromised people to change without making redemption automatic. Mel's revision of her judgment that Abigael is irredeemable is a key example of compassion as willingness to revise a moral verdict.
- **Compassion versus classify-and-contain justice** — The Perfecti classify beings as chaotic with little regard for context and punish through association. The sisters eventually reject the context-blind framework itself rather than merely seeking exemptions for friends.
- **Compassion versus coercive safety** — The series challenges peaceful-looking systems built on erased memory, lost consent, suppression of autonomy, or pre-emptive containment.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x04 Exorcise Your Demons** — Angela Wu is possessed by a catastrophic demon.
- **2x10 Curse Words** — Jordan faces punishment rooted in an ancestor's violence against witches.
- **3x13 Chaos Theory** — The Perfecti use classification and containment with little attention to context.
- **4x12 Be Kind. Rewind** — A seemingly peaceful magical order depends on erased memory and lost consent.
- **4x13 The End Is Never the End** — The finale continues the themes of apology, sacrifice, sisterhood, sanctuary, and community repair.

<a id="work-dc-s-legends-of-tomorrow"></a>
### DC's Legends of Tomorrow

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 89.6  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 87.3  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt4532368/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legends_of_Tomorrow)

#### Synopsis

A changing band of misfits tries to repair history.

#### Plot overview

A changing band of misfits tries to repair history. The story especially follows Sara Lance, who moves from assassin and reluctant team member to a captain who repeatedly builds belonging for people conventional heroic systems reject. Alongside Mick Rory, the plot develops through a violent criminal gradually becomes loyal teammate, writer, father and protector; the team's refusal to reduce him to his worst behavior is central.

#### Compassion analysis

Legends begins as a team of expendable misfits assembled because history supposedly does not need them and gradually turns that insult into a moral identity: people dismissed as villains, screwups, monsters, fugitives or temporal mistakes repeatedly become family and protect one another. Its comedy is extreme, but its compassion is often serious underneath the absurdity, especially around redemption, magical-creature persecution, free will, grief and the right to become different from the role history assigned.

**Why it matches:** It aligns strongly with The Good Place's serious ethics under comedy and Charmed/Legacies-style redemption. Nora, Astra, Mick, Snart and even artificial Gideon repeatedly demonstrate that former villain, demon-raised child, criminal, robot or magical creature is not a final moral category. The later seasons also explicitly reject benevolent systems that remove free will for safety.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~433 events · ~1386 compassion-focused minutes · ~30% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The team is built from people history calls insignificant** — Rip initially recruits people partly because their deaths will not matter to the timeline. The series gradually rejects that premise by making their relationships and choices historically and morally consequential.
- **Former villains become genuine family members** — Mick Rory, Leonard Snart, Nora Darhk and Astra all move from antagonist/criminal identities toward self-sacrifice, service and belonging.
- **Redemption requires opportunities, not moral purity** — Characters often improve because the team continues giving them roles and trust after ordinary institutions would only punish them.
- **Nora Darhk is not defined by possession or her father** — Her early villainy is tied to Mallus and Damien; later she voluntarily faces punishment and becomes a fairy godmother helping children.
- **Astra's hell upbringing is treated as context rather than essence** — John and the team repeatedly frame Astra as someone shaped by abandonment and Hell, not as inherently damned.
- **Magical creatures are reclassified from threats to persons** — Season four moves from Bureau containment toward friendship and public defense of creatures initially treated as monsters to imprison or send to Hell.
- **Mona's compassion exposes institutional cruelty** — Her insistence that magical prisoners have feelings and needs turns a containment system into a moral issue rather than a logistical one.
- **Free will is morally preferable to comfortable control** — The Fates' perfect world supplies order and safety at the cost of choice; the Legends reject that bargain as fundamentally wrong.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **History is sometimes treated instrumentally** — The team can accept suffering as a fixed point in one episode while breaking rules for loved ones in another, creating moral inconsistency.
- **Main characters receive more redemption opportunities** — Named villains often get context and second chances that disposable historical antagonists do not.
- **Time manipulation can override consent** — Memory alteration, timeline changes and resurrection are sometimes performed for benevolent reasons without full autonomy.
- **Comedy can minimize harm** — Serious trauma, death and coercion occasionally become punchlines or are emotionally resolved faster than their stakes suggest.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x16 Legendary** — A team originally recruited as expendable chooses to continue protecting history after Rip's original revenge mission ends.
- **2x09 Raiders of the Lost Art** — Mick continues carrying grief over Snart's sacrifice.
- **3x09 Beebo the God of War** — Leo Snart offers his life so others may live, extending Snart's redemption pattern into an alternate version.
- **4x06 Tender Is the Nate** — Nora explicitly accepts that she should answer for her past while Ray insists she can still become better.
- **4x16 Hey, World!** — The team's solution to fear of magical creatures is public contact rather than extermination or permanent imprisonment.
- **5x14 The One Where We're Trapped on TV** — The Fates create a painless controlled reality.

<a id="work-dark-matter-2015"></a>
### Dark Matter (2015)

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 89.4  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 87.2  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt4159076/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_Matter_(2015_TV_series))

#### Synopsis

An amnesiac spacecraft crew discovers troubling past identities.

#### Plot overview

An amnesiac spacecraft crew discovers troubling past identities. The story especially follows Two / Portia Lin, who begins as pragmatic leader with a feared criminal identity and becomes protector of a fragile chosen family, repeatedly choosing rescue and solidarity over self-preservation. Alongside Three / Marcus Boone, the plot develops through abrasive mercenary presentation gradually reveals grief, loyalty and tenderness; he becomes a major example of judging action rather than persona.

#### Compassion analysis

Dark Matter's entire premise is a compassion experiment: six apparent criminals wake without autobiographical memory and must decide whether the atrocities attached to their names determine who they are now. The series repeatedly answers that present choices matter, but it does not erase victims or consequences. The crew becomes family by protecting one another while confronting histories they did not consciously choose to inherit.

**Why it matches:** This is one of the closest matches to the verified profile's redemption and personhood themes. Like Charmed and Legacies, identity categories—criminal, killer, android, clone, corporate enemy—are repeatedly shown to be insufficient. Like The Good Place, moral improvement happens through repeated relationships and opportunities rather than a single conversion.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~153 events · ~520 compassion-focused minutes · ~31% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Past identity is not destiny** — The crew awaken with reputations as killers and criminals but no memories. Their new decisions quickly diverge from the people the galaxy expects them to be.
- **Responsibility survives memory loss** — The series does not simply declare the past irrelevant. Characters must decide what they owe victims and whether to continue benefiting from former crimes.
- **The crew becomes family through reciprocal risk** — They repeatedly choose one another over profit, escape or strategic safety despite beginning as strangers who distrust each other.
- **Five is protected because she is family, not useful cargo** — As the youngest and most vulnerable crew member, Five's safety becomes a recurring moral priority even when her memory access is strategically valuable.
- **The Android's personhood expands over time** — She begins as a ship system and develops preferences, identity, emotion-like experience, friendships and bodily autonomy; the crew increasingly treats those changes as hers to choose.
- **Artificial consciousness is not lesser consciousness** — Android upgrades and the existence of other android communities turn her arc from exceptional machine to a broader rights/personhood issue.
- **Three's apparent selfishness repeatedly gives way to care** — His abrasive persona hides deep loyalty, grief and willingness to protect the crew, demonstrating that emotional presentation is not identical to moral character.
- **Six's guilt becomes political responsibility** — His past as a revolutionary/terrorist and later choices push him toward protecting exploited populations rather than retreating into private remorse.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The crew still kills frequently** — The redemption ethic exists inside a violent space-opera structure, and some enemies receive far less contextual compassion than main characters.
- **Memory loss creates moral asymmetry** — It is easier for viewers and characters to separate new selves from crimes when the guilty memories are literally absent.
- **Loved characters receive more second chances** — Ryo and others benefit from personal history with the crew in ways anonymous adversaries often do not.
- **Android autonomy is sometimes subordinated to mission need** — The crew grows toward recognizing her personhood, but she is still periodically treated as a system whose body/program can be modified for tactical reasons.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x01-1x02 opening arc** — The crew learns their public identities are violent criminals.
- **1x08 Episode Eight** — The crew confronts information about prior atrocities and personal identities.
- **2x03 I've Seen the Other Side of You** — Behavioral/memory changes make familiar crewmates act like prior selves.
- **2x06 We Should Have Seen This Coming** — Characters explicitly contrast endless revenge with the possibility of letting go.
- **3x01 Being Better is So Much Harder** — The crew uses nonlethal force in a collapsing political situation.
- **3x09 Isn't That a Paradox?** — The Android remains conscious while hacked and unable to control actions, separating imposed behavior from moral identity.

<a id="work-doctor-who"></a>
### Doctor Who

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 82.7  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 83.5  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0436992/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_Who)

#### Synopsis

A time traveller encounters endangered lives throughout history and the universe.

#### Plot overview

A time traveller encounters endangered lives throughout history and the universe. The story especially follows The Doctor, who repeated regenerations reinterpret the same chosen identity: someone trying to convert war trauma, loneliness, and immense power into rescue, curiosity, restraint, and mercy. Alongside Rose Tyler, the plot develops through frequently humanizes people and creatures the Doctor risks treating abstractly, grounding cosmic choices in individual lives.

#### Compassion analysis

Across eras, Doctor Who repeatedly defines heroism as the attempt to save rather than destroy, understand rather than categorize, and interrupt cycles of vengeance. The ethic is inconsistent across hundreds of stories, but at its strongest the Doctor's identity is explicitly tied to mercy, kindness, refusal of genocide, and belief that enemies may still be reachable.

**Why it matches:** 

**Occurrence estimate:** ~1927 events · ~6360 compassion-focused minutes · ~24% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The Doctor repeatedly defines self against genocide** — Stories from classic through modern eras make exterminating an enemy species a moral boundary the Doctor resists even when the enemy is dangerous.
- **Mercy can extend to the creator of one's worst enemy** — The Doctor saves young Davros and explicitly concludes that friends/enemies matter less than preserving mercy.
- **War is framed as a cycle to be broken** — The Zygon Inversion turns the Doctor's Time War trauma into an argument for negotiation, forgiveness, and refusing retaliatory escalation.
- **Compassion can reveal the nonviolent solution** — In The Beast Below, Amy realizes the Star Whale is helping voluntarily because it cannot bear children's suffering; recognizing kindness solves the crisis without mutilating the creature.
- **Everybody living is treated as triumph** — The Doctor Dances makes saving every victim emotionally extraordinary precisely because the Doctor is accustomed to impossible loss.
- **The enemy inside the friend / friend inside the enemy** — The series often destabilizes moral categories by showing personhood within Daleks, Cybermen, Zygons, aliens, monsters, and apparent threats.
- **History's victims remain individuals** — Episodes such as Vincent and the Doctor and Demons of the Punjab focus on people whose suffering cannot simply be 'fixed,' requiring companionship, witness, and dignity rather than heroic cure.
- **Kindness is explicitly distinguished from niceness** — The Twelfth Doctor's final instruction—never cruel, never cowardly, and always kind—summarizes a recurring ethic that kindness may require difficult action rather than pleasant demeanor.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The ethic is inconsistent across a huge corpus** — Some stories use disposable monsters or mass destruction with little reflection, while others make mercy central.
- **The Doctor can be coercive and paternalistic** — Memory manipulation, unilateral decisions, deception, and companion endangerment complicate the healer identity.
- **Mercy can depend heavily on the Doctor's personal judgment** — The series sometimes replaces institutional accountability with one extraordinary individual's moral discretion.
- **Companions often bear the cost of the Doctor's choices** — Compassion for strangers can coexist with neglect, secrecy, or manipulation toward close companions.

#### Representative evidence

- **The Doctor Dances** — The crisis ends with every transformed victim restored.
- **The Doctor's Daughter** — The Doctor explicitly rejects genocide as a definition of peace.
- **The Beast Below** — Humanity has tortured the Star Whale under the assumption that coercion is necessary.
- **Vincent and the Doctor** — The Doctor and Amy cannot cure Vincent's depression or rewrite his fate.
- **The Girl Who Waited** — Older Amy's existence is treated as morally real even though saving younger Amy would erase her timeline.
- **The Rings of Akhaten** — The Doctor refuses to let a child define being consumed by a parasite as sacred duty.

<a id="work-doom-patrol"></a>
### Doom Patrol

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 94.0  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 85.7  
**Vibe class:** TONAL OUTLIER  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt8416494/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doom_Patrol_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

Traumatized outsiders with disruptive abilities struggle to live together.

#### Plot overview

Traumatized outsiders with disruptive abilities struggle to live together. The story especially follows Jane / Kay / the Underground, who moves from survival through rigid internal roles toward greater integration and Kay's agency, with the team learning that care requires respecting multiplicity rather than demanding simplicity. Alongside Rita Farr, the plot develops through moves from shame and vanity toward leadership, reparative responsibility and finally the insight that love may require letting the group separate.

#### Compassion analysis

Doom Patrol is one of the library's deepest found-family/trauma works. Its protagonists are disabled, traumatized, queer, dissociative, monstrous-looking, aging or otherwise alienated people whose powers are inseparable from wounds. Compassion rarely means fixing them. It means making space for impossible interior lives, apologizing for harm, respecting boundaries, refusing shame, and eventually recognizing when the group's own codependence is preventing growth.

**Why it matches:** It is close to Haibane Renmei and Being Human in treating damaged people as morally worthy without pretending damage makes them harmless. Like The Good Place, it is often wildly comic while being serious about responsibility. Like Charmed/Legacies, identity labels—monster, freak, personality, robot, negative spirit—do not settle personhood.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~207 events · ~828 compassion-focused minutes · ~36% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The team is made of people society treats as broken** — Their physical and psychological conditions are not temporary obstacles to conventional heroism; they are the reality within which relationships must be built.
- **Jane's plurality is treated as a system of persons, not a gimmick** — The Underground gives individual identities, needs and protective functions to alters rather than reducing them to symptoms.
- **Kay's autonomy is distinct from Jane's role** — The series increasingly asks what Kay herself wants rather than assuming the primary personality should control the system indefinitely.
- **Trauma explains dysfunction without erasing harm** — Cliff, Rita, Larry, Jane, Vic and Rouge all hurt people; backstory creates context, not exemption.
- **Danny offers sanctuary without normalization** — Danny the Street becomes a refuge where queer and socially rejected people can be safe without making themselves legible to hostile norms.
- **Larry's queer history is inseparable from shame and missed care** — His arc moves from repression and self-hatred toward relationships with the Negative Spirit, Keeg and a more honest understanding of love.
- **Rita's leadership grows from accountability** — She moves from self-protective vanity toward responsibility, then ultimately recognizes that keeping the Doom Patrol intact may be harming everyone.
- **Cliff's abrasive love becomes increasingly explicit** — His vulgarity coexists with genuine care for Jane, Clara, Rory and the team; he slowly learns to show care without using anger as the only emotional language.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The group is deeply codependent** — They often enable avoidance and harm in one another, making found family both solution and problem.
- **Niles's 'care' is profoundly manipulative** — He rescues people while hiding that he caused or exploited their suffering, demonstrating how paternalistic benevolence can become abuse.
- **Trauma can dominate identity** — The series intentionally centers wounds, but this sometimes leaves limited space for characters outside therapeutic self-examination.
- **Violence and grotesque comedy can blunt empathy** — The tone occasionally turns other suffering into spectacle even while main characters receive deep interiority.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x07 Therapy Patrol** — The team attempts group therapy because their powers and conflicts are inseparable from unresolved trauma.
- **1x08 Danny Patrol** — Danny provides a living refuge for queer people and outsiders.
- **1x09 Jane Patrol** — The Underground is presented as a complex internal community formed to protect Kay from abuse.
- **2x08 Dad Patrol** — Cliff's relationship to his daughter and Niles's relationship to Dorothy force both men to confront what parental protection has cost their children.
- **3x05 Dada Patrol** — The Sisterhood and Rita's past foreground institutional rejection of people considered abnormal.
- **3x10 Amends Patrol** — The season's endpoint emphasizes apology and attempts to repair relationships after betrayal rather than simple defeat of an enemy.

<a id="work-enemy-mine"></a>
### Enemy Mine

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 95.3  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 91.6  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0089092/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enemy_Mine_(film))

#### Synopsis

Enemy pilots are stranded together on a hostile planet.

#### Plot overview

Enemy pilots are stranded together on a hostile planet. The story especially follows Willis Davidge, who moves from casually racist fighter pilot to friend, adoptive parent and protector willing to fight humans rather than abandon Drac children. Alongside Jeriba 'Jerry' Shigan, the plot develops through moves from enemy combatant to intimate friend who trusts Davidge with child, lineage and cultural responsibility.

#### Compassion analysis

Enemy Mine is a concentrated enemy-humanization narrative. Davidge and Jeriba begin with species slurs and war propaganda, crash together, become mutually dependent, learn one another's language and culture, and eventually become family. The film's decisive move is that Davidge's obligation survives Jeriba's death: he raises Zammis, returns to human society changed, and risks himself to rescue Drac children from human exploitation.

**Why it matches:** It is almost a textbook match to the user's serious compassion preference: enemy species become persons through direct relationship, prejudice becomes shame, found family crosses biology, and compassion changes action all the way from survival cooperation to active resistance against one's own side.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~11 events · ~44 compassion-focused minutes · ~41% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **War begins from species abstraction** — Humans and Dracs use slurs and assume the other side is inherently hostile before Davidge and Jeriba know anything personal about each other.
- **Mutual survival creates the first moral opening** — Practical cooperation forces each to recognize intelligence, vulnerability and competence in the supposed enemy.
- **Language learning changes moral distance** — As communication improves, insults become jokes, stories and cultural exchange rather than enemy noise.
- **Religious/cultural understanding replaces ridicule** — Davidge initially mocks Drac scripture but later learns and recites it, eventually using it in Zammis's lineage ceremony.
- **Jeriba becomes family rather than tolerated ally** — Their bond moves beyond ceasefire into affection, teasing, protection and future-oriented responsibility.
- **Pregnancy/parenthood destroys the soldier binary** — Jeriba's pregnancy makes care, birth and lineage more important than species war.
- **Davidge accepts responsibility for Zammis** — His promise to Jeriba creates a parental obligation that survives the immediate survival crisis.
- **Grief converts prejudice into commitment** — Jeriba's death does not send Davidge back to war identity; it deepens his responsibility to the Drac child.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The miners are simplified human villains** — The later slave-rescue section is morally clear but less nuanced than the earlier mutual enemy-humanization.
- **Cross-species reconciliation is personalized** — The film shows what one relationship can do but does not deeply explore institutional peace between the two civilizations.
- **Jeriba's death makes Davidge the continuing protagonist** — The Drac character's sacrifice/death risks shifting the final emotional reward toward the human's moral growth.
- **Cultural understanding remains partial** — Davidge's learning is sincere but necessarily compressed and filtered through one relationship.

#### Representative evidence

- **Initial crash survival** — Davidge and Jeriba begin by trying to kill one another.
- **Shelter and food** — They learn each other's practical needs and save one another from environmental threats.
- **Language and scripture** — Davidge learns Drac language/culture and shifts from mockery toward respect.
- **Jeriba's pregnancy** — Davidge responds to an alien reproductive reality by becoming caregiver rather than recoiling from difference.
- **Jeriba's death** — Davidge stays with Jeriba through birth/death and accepts the promise to protect Zammis.
- **Davidge returns with Zammis** — He raises the Drac child and teaches/learns cultural practices rather than assimilating Zammis into human identity.

<a id="work-everything-everywhere-all-at-once"></a>
### Everything Everywhere All at Once

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 92.7  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 89.1  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt6710474/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everything_Everywhere_All_at_Once)

#### Synopsis

A strained family relationship expands into a multiversal crisis.

#### Plot overview

A strained family relationship expands into a multiversal crisis. The story especially follows Evelyn Wang, who moves from overwhelmed criticism and defensive aggression toward active empathy, learning to use attention rather than domination to respond to conflict. Alongside Waymond Wang, the plot develops through the film's moral anchor; his kindness is revealed as deliberate survival strategy, while his divorce request shows that gentleness and boundaries can coexist.

#### Compassion analysis

Everything Everywhere All at Once explicitly makes compassion the answer to nihilism. Evelyn initially experiences other people's needs as burdens and conflict as something to win. Waymond's insistence on kindness under uncertainty gives her a new strategy: use her multiversal knowledge to understand what each attacker lacks, then respond to that need instead of overpowering them. The same shift ultimately lets her reach Joy without denying Joy's depression or forcing her to feel grateful.

**Why it matches:** It is one of the strongest serious-compassion matches despite its comedy and action. Compassion directly changes combat, family relationships and the film's philosophical resolution. It strongly resembles The Good Place in treating kindness as serious moral practice rather than niceness.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~15 events · ~58 compassion-focused minutes · ~42% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Waymond's kindness is strength rather than passivity** — His cookies, openness and attempts to understand others repeatedly produce practical results that Evelyn's aggression cannot.
- **Kindness is especially necessary under uncertainty** — The film's central moral line ties compassion to not knowing what another person is going through.
- **Evelyn learns to perceive hidden needs** — Multiverse knowledge becomes morally useful when she uses it to understand attackers rather than exploit their weaknesses.
- **Combat is transformed into care** — The climax visually preserves action choreography while replacing injury with acts that give opponents something emotionally or relationally needed.
- **Joy's nihilism is treated as pain rather than villain essence** — Jobu Tupaki's violence is contextualized by unbearable multiplicity, isolation and the belief that nothing can matter.
- **Evelyn refuses to let love become forced optimism** — Her final conversation with Joy does not prove life is objectively meaningful; she chooses relationship even knowing pain and absurdity remain.
- **Intergenerational rejection is consciously interrupted** — Evelyn recognizes that she has repeated Gong Gong's conditional acceptance toward Joy and Becky, then changes how she speaks and acts.
- **Becky is recognized rather than tolerated** — The family movement is from concealment/embarrassment toward openly naming Joy's partner as family.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Evelyn causes extensive physical harm before the compassionate turn** — The film intentionally stages moral change late, after considerable violence.
- **Joy's depression is resolved symbolically rather than clinically** — The family reconciliation is emotionally powerful but should not be read as a realistic complete treatment for suicidal depression.
- **Evelyn's new empathy is supernaturally enhanced** — Multiverse access gives her knowledge of needs ordinary people could not possess so quickly.
- **Family love can still feel possessive** — The climax deliberately leaves tension between holding on to Joy and respecting her wish for distance.

#### Representative evidence

- **IRS opening** — Waymond tries warmth, cookies and personal connection while Evelyn dismisses this as weakness.
- **Movie-star universe conversation** — Waymond explains that kindness is how he fights and survives, not evidence that he is naïve.
- **Waymond's plea during violence** — He asks everyone to stop fighting because people are scared and confused and urges kindness precisely because no one fully knows what is happening.
- **Evelyn's transformed fight** — Instead of exploiting opponents' weaknesses, Evelyn uses multiversal knowledge to meet needs and disarm conflict emotionally.
- **Deirdre confrontation** — Evelyn recognizes Deirdre's loneliness and tells her she is lovable rather than simply defeating her.
- **Joy and the bagel** — Evelyn approaches Jobu's wish to disappear as despair rather than evil.

<a id="work-extraordinary"></a>
### Extraordinary

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 71.5  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 73.9  
**Vibe class:** MODERATE / SELECTIVE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt14531842/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extraordinary_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

A young woman lacks powers in a society where nearly everyone has them.

#### Plot overview

A young woman lacks powers in a society where nearly everyone has them. The story especially follows Jen, who moves from power-shame and self-centered coping toward intermittent but real accountability, especially when forced to recognize that being sad does not entitle her to hurt others. Alongside Carrie, the plot develops through a chronic helper whose power literalizes emotional labor; she learns that kindness without boundaries can erase her own identity.

#### Compassion analysis

Extraordinary is much less heroic than most speculative entries: its compassion is about damaged young adults learning not to use shame, grief, powers or emotional need as excuses to treat one another badly. The superpower premise makes social comparison literal. Jen's lack of a power creates deep shame, while Carrie's power makes her too useful to other people, Kash's lets him avoid consequences, and Jizzlord's transformation disrupts identity and family. Compassion emerges as honest accountability rather than unconditional niceness.

**Why it matches:** It resembles The Good Place in using comedy to treat moral growth seriously. It is less rescue-oriented than Charmed/Legacies, but strong on the user's preference for compassion with depth: grief, shame, boundaries, accepting consequence, and refusing to define a person by ability or usefulness.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~48 events · ~130 compassion-focused minutes · ~27% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Powerlessness is treated as stigma** — Jen's lack of a power becomes a source of shame and social inferiority; the series gradually exposes how much of her cruelty and desperation comes from believing she is lesser.
- **Having a power can also be dehumanizing** — Carrie's ability to channel the dead makes other people treat her as service infrastructure for their grief rather than a person with limits.
- **Compassion requires boundaries** — Carrie repeatedly learns that helping everyone can erase her own needs, while the group gradually learns that access to her power is not an entitlement.
- **Jen must learn responsibility rather than self-pity** — Her pain is real, but season two increasingly refuses to let sadness excuse betrayal, jealousy or impulsive harm.
- **Accountability is framed as emotionally mature care** — Jen explicitly recognizes that hurting someone means facing consequences rather than inventing an equivalent punishment that will make the relationship even.
- **Jizzlord's identity is not reducible to his transformation** — His cat-to-human history makes him vulnerable, dependent and confused; later family revelations force others to respect an identity not centered on Jen.
- **Love does not create ownership** — Jen's relationship with Jizzlord is repeatedly tested by the fact that he has a past and family outside the role she prefers him to occupy.
- **Carrie's grief-work power exposes unequal emotional labor** — Because she can speak for the dead, people repeatedly push responsibility for closure onto her, highlighting the cost of being the compassionate one.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Characters can be casually cruel** — The comedy depends heavily on selfishness and humiliation, so compassion is often corrective rather than the baseline tone.
- **Accountability sometimes becomes comic retaliation** — Characters occasionally answer hurt with deliberately petty harm, blurring the distinction between honest consequences and revenge.
- **Powers create consent problems** — Channeling the dead, time manipulation and other abilities can intrude on privacy or agency, and the show does not always explore these implications deeply.
- **Compassion is mostly intimate** — The series is strong on friendship and family but weak on broad community or structural compassion.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x06 The Real Powers Are the Friends We Made Along the Way** — Jen and Carrie weaponize new friendships during a conflict, exposing how easily loneliness becomes possessiveness.
- **1x08 Surprise!** — Relationship crises reveal how much the group relies on one another despite constant mockery.
- **2x03 The Exorcism of Carrie Jackson** — Carrie's power makes death and grief an everyday demand placed on her.
- **2x05 Meet the Parent** — Jizzlord's previous family life complicates Jen's preferred narrative of who he is.
- **2x06 Jen V Nora: Ultimate Showdown** — Jen tries to compete with Nora rather than listen to what Jizzlord needs.
- **2x07 Be the Daddy You Want to See in the World** — Jen explicitly recognizes that hurting someone creates consequences rather than entitlement to symmetrical retaliation.

<a id="work-farscape"></a>
### Farscape

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 88.0  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 84.0  
**Vibe class:** MODERATE / SELECTIVE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0187636/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farscape)

#### Synopsis

Escaped prisoners of different species share a living spacecraft.

#### Plot overview

Escaped prisoners of different species share a living spacecraft. The story especially follows John Crichton, who moves from displaced human assuming Earth norms to member of an alien family who will threaten every empire in the galaxy before accepting genocide or abandoning people he loves. Alongside Aeryn Sun, the plot develops through one of the strongest deprogramming arcs in science fiction: a Peacekeeper taught that compassion is weakness becomes partner, mother and fiercely protective member of a multi-species family.

#### Compassion analysis

Farscape begins with people from species and political systems that distrust one another trapped on a living ship and slowly turns them into family. Its compassion is messy and hard-earned: Aeryn must unlearn Peacekeeper species hierarchy, Pilot and Moya insist on being treated as persons rather than transport, Rygel and Chiana remain selfish while becoming loyal, and Crichton learns that humanity is neither the center nor moral benchmark of the universe. The Peacekeeper Wars culminates in forcing two imperial powers to experience the cost of total war until they accept peace.

**Why it matches:** It is a strong match to Charmed, Legacies, Sanctuary and Voyager: personhood across species, found family among former enemies, conditioning versus chosen identity, morally complicated redemption and refusal to treat alien difference as inferiority. It is darker and more ethically chaotic than those titles, which makes its compassion feel earned rather than automatic.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~299 events · ~1135 compassion-focused minutes · ~28% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Moya is a person, not a ship** — Her biological needs, pregnancy, fear and preferences become moral considerations for the crew rather than logistical facts.
- **Pilot is a person, not an interface** — Episodes repeatedly expose how Peacekeepers and even Moya's crew can instrumentalize Pilot's body and labor; trust requires respecting his distinct agency.
- **Aeryn unlearns species hierarchy** — She begins believing Sebacean Peacekeepers are superior and gradually forms intimate family bonds with species she was trained to regard as lesser.
- **Conditioning does not fix moral destiny** — Aeryn's entire arc demonstrates that a person raised inside authoritarian military ideology can become different through relationship and experience.
- **The crew's family includes people who remain selfish** — Rygel and Chiana do not become conventionally virtuous, but loyalty and care become real without erasing personality.
- **Past cruelty can be confronted inside relationship** — The Way We Weren't forces Aeryn to face her participation in Pilot's traumatic past while preserving the possibility of present trust.
- **Crichton becomes less human-centric** — His initial habit of treating Earth assumptions as universal is repeatedly corrected by alien perspectives.
- **Returning to Earth reveals reciprocal prejudice** — Terra Firma shows humans reacting to alien companions with suspicion and objectification, forcing Crichton to recognize that 'home' is morally less simple than memory.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The crew can be brutally self-interested** — They lie, steal, betray and threaten one another; compassion is a growth trajectory rather than stable baseline.
- **Scorpius receives extensive context despite severe abuse** — Understanding his history risks overshadowing the scale of harm he inflicts on Crichton and others.
- **Violence is pervasive** — The series' anti-war and cross-species empathy coexist with frequent lethal action.
- **Sexual and bodily autonomy are repeatedly violated** — Mind control, neural chips, forced medical procedures and cultural practices create consent problems that the series sometimes uses mainly as plot engines.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x22 Family Ties** — The title crystallizes the season's shift from accidental fugitives to people willing to risk themselves for one another.
- **2x05 The Way We Weren't** — Aeryn's past participation in Pilot's trauma is exposed.
- **3x05 Different Destinations** — The crew attempts to save people from a historical massacre because watching suffering feels morally intolerable.
- **3x17 The Choice** — Aeryn's grief after Crichton's death is given sustained space rather than immediately converted into action plot.
- **4x13 Terra Firma** — Crichton returns to Earth with alien companions and sees human suspicion directed at the people who became his family.
- **4x17 A Constellation of Doubt** — Human public reactions to alien contact expose prejudice, fear and fascination.

<a id="work-fringe"></a>
### Fringe

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 92.1  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 88.9  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt1119644/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fringe_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

Investigators uncover experiments linking extraordinary phenomena and parallel worlds.

#### Plot overview

Investigators uncover experiments linking extraordinary phenomena and parallel worlds. The story especially follows Walter Bishop, who from brilliant scientist driven by grief and entitlement toward a father who understands the damage he caused and ultimately sacrifices his own future to repair it. Alongside Olivia Dunham, the plot develops through a childhood experiment survivor who nevertheless repeatedly chooses empathy, including toward alternate-universe counterparts and frightened anomalies.

#### Compassion analysis

Fringe evolves from procedural body-horror into one of the strongest compassion-and-reparation stories in the library. Walter's original act of love—stealing Peter from another universe—causes catastrophic harm, and the series spends years turning private grief into responsibility for an entire other world. The two universes move from enemy abstraction toward mutual recognition, while Observers themselves become a later test of whether emotion and care can arise in beings assumed to be incapable of them.

**Why it matches:** This is an exceptional match to Charmed, The Good Place and Nausicaä. Compassion changes systems and cosmology: an 'enemy universe' becomes a community worth saving; Walter's guilt becomes sacrifice and repair; September learns fatherhood; Peter and Olivia repeatedly choose connection over revenge; and apparent monsters/experiments are often revealed as victims of science or circumstance.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~351 events · ~1333 compassion-focused minutes · ~31% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Walter's central arc is responsibility for harm caused by love** — He crossed universes to save a child, but compassion for one family created catastrophe for another world. His growth is learning that loving motive does not cancel responsibility.
- **Reparation matters more than self-forgiveness** — Walter repeatedly seeks ways to repair damage rather than merely feel absolved for it.
- **The alternate universe becomes morally equal** — Characters initially speak of the other side as invaders or copies; sustained contact reveals families, losses, loyalties and people equally convinced they are defending home.
- **Enemy leaders are humanized** — Walternate's brutality is contextualized by a universe collapsing from damage Walter caused, without making all of his actions acceptable.
- **Fauxlivia becomes more than an impostor** — Her relationships, motherhood and loyalties make her a full person rather than a counterfeit Olivia.
- **Peter is not property of either father or universe** — His childhood abduction exposes how parental love can violate autonomy. Adult Peter must define belonging for himself.
- **Cortexiphan victims reveal the cost of instrumental science** — Walter and Bell's experiments on children become a persistent moral debt, especially in Olivia's relationship to Walter.
- **White Tulip links forgiveness to moral change, not erasure** — Walter seeks a sign that forgiveness might be possible while still intending to confess the truth; the episode does not let remorse substitute for honesty.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Fringe Division sometimes violates subjects' autonomy** — Emergency science and security work can reproduce the same instrumental logic the series critiques.
- **Body-horror victims can be proceduralized** — Some episodic victims exist mainly to drive mystery rather than receive sustained person-centered attention.
- **Walter's charm can soften audience judgment** — The series works hard to preserve love for Walter while confronting enormous harm, and some consequences receive more emotional than legal accountability.
- **Alternate-universe reconciliation is politically simplified** — A conflict produced by years of devastation resolves more cleanly than comparable real-world trauma would.

#### Representative evidence

- **2x18 White Tulip** — Walter's guilt over stealing Peter is explicitly connected to whether he can be forgiven.
- **2x22 Over There, Part 1** — The alternate universe ceases to be abstract as the team meets counterparts with families and histories.
- **3x01 Olivia** — Alternate-universe authorities try to overwrite Olivia's identity.
- **3x09 Marionette** — Olivia's return is not treated as simple restoration; emotional violation and the consequences of Fauxlivia's impersonation are given space.
- **3x22 The Day We Died** — Peter sees a future where one universe was destroyed to save the other.
- **4x09 Enemy of My Enemy** — Both Fringe divisions cooperate against a shared threat.

<a id="work-fruits-basket-2019"></a>
### Fruits Basket (2019)

**Flags:** `Anime` `Animation`  
**Concept:** 96.6  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 92.4  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt9304350/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fruits_Basket_(2019_TV_series))

#### Synopsis

An outsider becomes close to a family constrained by a curse.

#### Plot overview

An outsider becomes close to a family constrained by a curse. The story especially follows Tohru Honda, who an orphan whose instinctive care creates safety for others, then gradually learns that her own grief, desire and need to be chosen are equally legitimate. Alongside Kyo Sohma, the plot develops through moves from rage and self-loathing toward accepting love and a future, without the story pretending his guilt disappears simply because someone cares.

#### Compassion analysis

Fruits Basket is one of the most compassion-saturated long dramas in the library. Tohru's kindness matters because it gives abused and isolated people a relationship that does not demand they perform family roles, zodiac roles or emotional usefulness. The series' strongest idea is not that compassion magically fixes trauma, but that being seen without judgment gives people enough safety to make choices the Sohma system previously made impossible.

**Why it matches:** It closely matches Good Witch's non-coercive help, The Good Place's relational moral growth and Charmed/Legacies' rejection of inherited identity. The supernatural curse matters, but the emotional system around it—abuse, isolation, obligation and fear—is the deeper antagonist.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~208 events · ~665 compassion-focused minutes · ~44% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Tohru notices the person hidden by role** — Zodiac members are treated by the household as people before they are animals, family assets or extensions of Akito.
- **Kindness is not the same as pity** — The series repeatedly worries over whether care is condescending; Tohru's strongest relationships work because she treats vulnerability as compatible with dignity.
- **Kyo's self-hatred is not validated as moral truth** — His Cat status, mother's death and guilt over Kyoko drive the belief that he should be confined or rejected; Tohru's care does not accept that conclusion.
- **Yuki's healing depends on non-romantic recognition** — Tohru becomes a maternal/safe figure rather than a prize, allowing him to develop relationships and identity outside trauma.
- **Momiji models compassion without possession** — He repeatedly supports Tohru's happiness even when it excludes him, making care compatible with grief and unreturned love.
- **Rin demonstrates that victims need not forgive abusers** — The ending preserves her anger at Akito rather than making universal forgiveness a moral requirement.
- **Akito is contextualized as both victim and victimizer** — The adults and family structure created her entitlement and terror of abandonment, but the series still presents her as responsible for severe abuse.
- **Tohru reaches Akito through shared fear of abandonment** — Their decisive connection occurs not because Tohru excuses violence but because she recognizes the terror underneath Akito's collapsing identity.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Akito's social reintegration is rapid relative to the harm** — The final arc sometimes moves from accountability to acceptance more quickly than the scale of abuse would realistically support.
- **Tohru can over-function as emotional healer** — Her caregiving risks implying one exceptionally kind person can absorb a family's systemic trauma.
- **Romantic resolution sometimes substitutes for broader support** — Several arcs settle into couples without fully exploring continuing therapeutic or communal needs.
- **The curse can externalize abuse too neatly** — Supernatural bonds provide a dramatic metaphor, but real family abuse cannot be broken by one magical dissolution.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x05 I've Been Fooling Myself** — Tohru tries to leave the Sohma home rather than presume she belongs.
- **1x24 Let's Go Home** — Kyo's true form is treated by others as shameful and horrifying.
- **2x18 Wanna Kiss?** — Rin's isolation and abuse history are gradually disclosed.
- **3x02 That's an Unwavering Truth** — Tohru worries about prying into painful personal history and repeatedly hesitates because she does not want concern to become intrusion.
- **3x08 I'm Disappointed in You** — Kyo confesses guilt around Kyoko's death and rejects Tohru's love because he sees himself as morally contaminating.
- **3x09 What's Your Name?** — Akito expects abandonment once the curse/bonds collapse.
