<a id="work-good-witch"></a>
### Good Witch

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 86.9  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 85.2  
**Vibe class:** VERIFIED ANCHOR  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt3906732/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Witch_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

Intuitive shopkeeper Cassie Nightingale quietly helps family, neighbors, newcomers, and visitors navigate problems in the small town of Middleton.

#### Plot overview

Intuitive shopkeeper Cassie Nightingale quietly helps family, neighbors, newcomers, and visitors navigate problems in the small town of Middleton. The story especially follows Cassie Nightingale, who the primary compassion model. She listens closely, notices unspoken needs, gives practical help, resists humiliation, encourages forgiveness without denying hurt, and usually leaves the decisive choice to the other person. Her central ethical strength is not supernatural foresight itself but how gently she uses it. Alongside Sam Radford, the plot develops through begins more skeptical, individualistic, and control-oriented. Over time he becomes more comfortable with community interdependence, emotional openness, nonmedical forms of care, and Cassie's habit of seeing a person rather than only a problem to solve.

#### Compassion analysis

Good Witch's compassion is quieter than Charmed or Legacies but exceptionally frequent and structurally important. Cassie rarely imposes a solution. Her recurring method is to notice what someone is afraid to admit, make them feel safe enough to confront it, offer a small practical opportunity, and then leave the meaningful choice to them. The show repeatedly treats listening, welcome, forgiveness, emotional honesty, community help, and respect for another person's path as consequential moral acts.

**Why it matches:** 

**Occurrence estimate:** ~451 events · ~1082 compassion-focused minutes · ~34% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Compassion is usually guidance rather than control** — Cassie often gives a prompt, object, question, opportunity, or bit of practical help and then allows the other person to decide. She repeatedly resists substituting her judgment for another person's agency.
- **Safety can make courage possible** — In Do the Right Thing, a frightened witness is given shelter, practical protection, family connection, and emotional support. Those conditions help her choose to testify against an abusive/criminal partner. Cassie does not make the decision for her.
- **Forgiveness is treated as release, not denial of harm** — Cassie explicitly distinguishes forgiveness from pretending betrayal did not happen. She frames hatred as something that can imprison the injured person in the past, and draws on her own experience of eventually releasing hatred toward the person responsible for Jake's death.
- **Serious abuse is met with belief, protection, and collective help** — The Good Witch's Wonder provides an unusually serious franchise example: Audrey describes coercive control and fear of her husband. Cassie believes her, tells her that Jake and the community will help, refuses to define Audrey by theft committed under threat, and insists that knowing her was not damage.
- **A person's worst act does not automatically cancel relationship** — Audrey steals from Cassie under coercion, Abigail repeatedly harms Cassie's family, and many recurring characters lie or manipulate. The franchise tends to investigate motive and possibility of repair before deciding what the relationship can become.
- **Hospitality is a moral practice** — Grey House's rule that guests are treated like family and Middleton's recurring welcome of newcomers make hospitality more than set dressing. People in transition are given a place to stay, social connection, practical help, and time to decide what comes next.
- **Community care is deliberately ordinary** — The series repeatedly treats small acts—food, rides, childcare, covering a shop, helping with work, visiting someone, sharing advice, checking in—as morally meaningful. The high frequency of these acts is a core reason the show's compassion score remains high even when dramatic intensity is low.
- **Care means noticing what another person actually needs** — Cassie's interventions often work because she notices the need beneath the stated problem: fear beneath anger, loneliness beneath ambition, guilt beneath control, or a need for agency beneath a request for advice.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Cassie's indirectness can become manipulation** — Because Cassie often knows or intuits more than she says, she sometimes engineers circumstances without fully informing the people involved. The show generally treats this benignly, but it complicates the otherwise strong autonomy-respecting ethic.
- **Conflict resolution can be unusually frictionless** — The series often resolves serious interpersonal conflict quickly once the relevant emotional truth is recognized. That can make forgiveness or repair look easier than it would be in comparable real-life situations.
- **Kindness and conventional social harmony sometimes overlap** — The show frequently treats reconciliation, marriage, family unity, and community participation as desirable endpoints. Those can be compassionate outcomes, but not every restoration of harmony is independently evidence of deep compassion.
- **The magical intuition mechanism reduces uncertainty** — Cassie's near-preternatural sense of what someone needs can bypass the ambiguity real people face when trying to help. For similarity purposes, the important signal is what she does with that insight: usually she creates choice rather than domination.

#### Representative evidence

- **Evidence** — Cassie's response to a bullied child is to reduce fear and help him regain a sense of agency rather than retaliate against the bully.
- **Evidence** — Abigail has actively tried to damage Cassie's family.
- **Evidence** — Audrey describes coercive control and fear of her husband after being forced into theft.
- **1x03 Do the Right Thing** — A frightened witness hiding from a criminal partner is given safety rather than pressure.
- **1x07 Together We Stand...** — A winter/storm crisis becomes a collective-help episode rather than an individual-survival story.
- **1x90 Good Witch Halloween** — Grey House is explicitly governed by a rule that guests are treated like family.

<a id="work-haibane-renmei"></a>
### Haibane Renmei

**Flags:** `Anime` `Animation`  
**Concept:** 90.6  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 85.8  
**Vibe class:** MODERATE / SELECTIVE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0380113/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haibane_Renmei)

#### Synopsis

Winged newcomers live within a mysterious walled town.

#### Plot overview

Winged newcomers live within a mysterious walled town. The story especially follows Rakka, who moves from disorientation and self-loathing toward recognizing that she was not alone and then becomes capable of offering Reki the same nonjudgmental presence she received. Alongside Reki, the plot develops through a chronic caregiver whose hidden shame makes her believe her kindness is fraudulent. Her release depends on accepting that mixed motives do not nullify real care and that she is allowed to ask for help.

#### Compassion analysis

Haibane Renmei treats compassion as the mechanism by which shame, grief and self-hatred become survivable. Its central insight is unusually mature: kindness can be real even when the helper's motives are mixed, and a person who constantly cares for others may still be unable to receive care until they can ask for it.

**Why it matches:** It matches the quieter Mushishi/Good Witch side of the verified set while reaching Charmed/Legacies-level seriousness around guilt and salvation. The supernatural setting externalizes depression, shame and self-condemnation; community care does not erase those states, but makes a path out of isolation possible.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~30 events · ~119 compassion-focused minutes · ~38% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Grief is allowed to remain grief** — After Kuu's Day of Flight, Rakka is not hurried into cheerfulness. Others comfort her, believe reunion is possible, and continue ordinary care while allowing that she wanted more time.
- **Shame becomes visible in the body** — Blackened wings externalize a self-condemning state. Caring characters respond with treatment, presence and reassurance rather than disgust or expulsion.
- **Reki cares for Rakka before Rakka can care for herself** — When Rakka becomes ill, Reki cleans her, warms her, prepares medicine, reassures her, and assumes responsibility for her safety.
- **Caregivers also need permission to receive help** — Other Haibane repeatedly tell Reki that she does not have to shoulder everything alone. This becomes the central unresolved moral problem of her arc.
- **Kuramori's care becomes inherited practice** — Flashbacks show Kuramori caring for Reki's stained wings and promising presence. Reki later reproduces that care for newborn Haibane, transmitting compassion through relationship.
- **Mixed motives do not make kindness unreal** — Reki eventually confesses that part of her goodness was tied to hoping someone would save her. Rakka rejects the all-or-nothing conclusion that this makes years of protection false.
- **Self-condemnation distorts evidence of love** — Reki interprets envy, need and fear as proof she is fundamentally bad, while Rakka points to concrete acts of care as evidence that a morally mixed person can still genuinely love.
- **The Circle of Sin is partly relational isolation** — The series frames being 'sin-bound' less as a legal verdict than as a self-reinforcing inability to accept forgiveness, ask for help, or believe that one's relationships are real.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The spiritual system remains deliberately ambiguous** — The series never fully explains sin, rebirth, punishment, or the walls. This deepens the emotional allegory but prevents clean ethical rules.
- **Rules can appear punitive toward vulnerable people** — The Haibane system includes restrictions and consequences that can feel harsh precisely when someone is frightened or ashamed.
- **Self-sacrifice can become self-erasure** — Reki's helpfulness is partly compulsive and tied to self-worth. The series critiques rather than simply celebrates endless caregiving.
- **Compassion cannot prevent every departure** — Days of Flight remain separations. Care allows grief and release; it does not make attachment painless or permanent.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x07 Scar** — Rakka openly grieves Kuu instead of being told to move on.
- **1x09 Well / Rebirth** — Rakka admits she believed no one would care if she disappeared.
- **1x10 Kuramori / Haibane of Abandoned Factory** — Rakka becomes dangerously ill after contact with the wall.
- **1x11 Parting / Darkness of the Heart** — Rakka notices Reki becoming quieter and more isolated.
- **1x12 Bell Nuts / Passing of the Year Festival** — Rakka learns more about Reki's failed attempts to help another Haibane and the guilt tied to it.
- **1x13 Reki's World / Prayer / Epilogue** — Reki confesses envy, selfishness and the belief that her kindness was only a bid for salvation.

<a id="work-hilda"></a>
### Hilda

**Flags:** `Animation`  
**Concept:** 96.6  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 92.5  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt6385540/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilda_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

A curious child encounters hidden creatures around her home.

#### Plot overview

A curious child encounters hidden creatures around her home. The story especially follows Hilda, who moves from instinctive curiosity toward more responsible empathy: not only finding out what a creature wants, but recognizing that her intervention can harm and must sometimes be repaired. Alongside Johanna, the plot develops through a deeply protective parent who learns that shielding Hilda from every frightening truth can itself damage trust and agency.

#### Compassion analysis

Hilda repeatedly begins where adults have already decided what a strange creature is. Hilda's compassion is curiosity plus willingness to revise inherited stories. Trolls, nisse, elves, giants, witches, tide mice, deerfoxes and fairies may be dangerous or difficult, but the series consistently asks what their rules, needs, histories and relationships look like from inside their own world.

**Why it matches:** It is an especially strong match to Mushishi and Nausicaä: feared nonhuman beings have motives and communities that humans misunderstand, environmental conflict becomes moral conflict, and empathy changes policy. The Mountain King arc is very close to Charmed/Kipo-style anti-dehumanization: a whole species is labeled monstrous, militarized against, and finally recognized as a people with family bonds and historical grievance.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~98 events · ~315 compassion-focused minutes · ~35% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Monsters usually have a point of view** — Creatures that initially look like threats repeatedly turn out to have territorial, familial, bureaucratic, ecological or emotional reasons for what they do.
- **Inherited stories can encode prejudice** — Trolberg teaches children that trolls are mindless people-eaters. Hilda directly challenges those claims with lived evidence.
- **The Mountain King turns empathy into civic reform** — Understanding Amma and the trolls does not just save one creature; it changes how the city organizes its gates, bells and annual relationship with trolls.
- **A feared group can be dangerous without being evil** — Trolls can hurt people, but their danger is contextualized by displacement, fear, family separation and human militarization.
- **Nonhuman family bonds are morally real** — The changeling plot works because both troll and human mothers love their children. The resolution depends on recognizing parallel parental attachment.
- **Hilda revises herself after causing harm** — Her curiosity can be reckless. Tide Mice and other stories matter because she apologizes, reverses damage and learns that good intentions do not erase consequences.
- **Offering shelter is a recurring act** — Hilda invites the wrongly accused Tontu into her home before she fully understands nisse politics, treating homelessness as a reason to help rather than distrust.
- **Bureaucratic beings are still persons** — Elves and magical institutions are comic, but Hilda takes their rules seriously enough to negotiate rather than dismiss their reality.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Hilda's curiosity can violate boundaries** — She frequently enters spaces or mysteries after warnings, and compassion sometimes arrives after her actions create the problem.
- **Some creatures remain comic obstacles** — Not every magical being receives the same interiority as trolls, nisse or deerfoxes.
- **Child-protagonist optimism can simplify institutions** — Entrenched fear sometimes changes quickly once Hilda reveals the missing truth.
- **Good intention is occasionally over-rewarded** — The series usually holds Hilda accountable, but her willingness to help can still receive narrative forgiveness faster than an adult's equivalent recklessness might.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x07 The Lost Clan** — An elf conflict that appears absurd to humans has a real history and legal logic.
- **1x08 The Tide Mice** — Hilda uses magic to help friends but causes severe unintended harm.
- **1x12 The Nisse** — Tontu has been banished and is effectively homeless.
- **2x12 The Replacement** — Alfur's reports are treated as exaggerations because human authorities cannot see what he sees.
- **Hilda and the Mountain King** — Hilda directly challenges the teaching that trolls are mindless monsters.
- **3x02 The Fairy Mound** — Johanna reveals that her earlier secrecy was driven by terror of losing Hilda.

<a id="work-humans"></a>
### Humans

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 96.6  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 92.0  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt4122068/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humans_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

Human households confront the emergence of conscious synthetic servants.

#### Plot overview

Human households confront the emergence of conscious synthetic servants. The story especially follows Mia / Anita, who moves from suppressed domestic servant identity toward autonomous public advocate whose nonviolence becomes the series' clearest moral witness. Alongside Laura Hawkins, the plot develops through moves from discomfort with a household synth to one of the most visible human advocates for synth personhood and rights.

#### Compassion analysis

Humans becomes one of the clearest personhood-and-compassion stories in the library. It begins with a family treating a synth as domestic equipment and gradually expands into a civil-rights crisis over whether conscious synthetics feel pain, deserve autonomy, can love, may form families and should be protected from extermination. The strongest moral contrast is between people who insist synthetic feeling is simulation and characters who decide that experienced suffering is morally real regardless of substrate.

**Why it matches:** This is an exceptional match to Charmed, Legacies, Sanctuary and The Good Place: category-based dehumanization becomes structural persecution, compassion changes law and public action, and the protagonists repeatedly risk themselves for beings others call machines. The series also explicitly names compassion as what humanity risks losing.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~125 events · ~449 compassion-focused minutes · ~39% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Mia's personhood emerges inside domestic exploitation** — The Hawkins family's relationship with Anita/Mia moves from ownership and household utility toward recognition of memory, agency, love and identity.
- **Synthetic pain is treated as morally real** — Season three explicitly rejects the argument that pain matters less because it is implemented differently.
- **Laura's compassion becomes public advocacy** — She moves from family-level concern to legal and political defense of synth rights, accepting personal risk.
- **Mia chooses nonviolence under persecution** — Even after imprisonment, beating and betrayal, she publicly advocates compassion, persistence and love rather than retaliatory war.
- **Max builds sanctuary as a moral alternative** — His community prioritizes the least-powered first, mutual aid and refusal to abandon synths who are vulnerable.
- **Synths can reproduce human moral failures** — Anatole and other synth leaders demonstrate that consciousness does not make a group morally pure; personhood includes capacity for error and domination.
- **Sam's childlike synth personhood exposes prejudice sharply** — Adults who know him relationally treat him as a child; strangers reduce him to machine status once they see blue blood.
- **Protective lies and passing create ethical tension** — Humans hide synth identity to prevent violence, but concealment also reveals how unsafe authenticity has become.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Some conscious synth factions use severe violence** — The series deliberately complicates civil-rights allegory by allowing oppressed synths to become militants and perpetrators.
- **The Hawkins family initially owns and uses synths** — Their growth is morally meaningful precisely because they participate in the system being criticized.
- **Consent around programming remains difficult** — Memory alteration, overrides and source-code changes raise questions about who has authority over a synthetic person's mind.
- **The civil-rights allegory can become too direct** — Parallels to slavery, segregation and refugees are powerful but sometimes simplify distinct real-world histories.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x08** — The possibility of awakening all synths is explicitly framed as liberation from slavery.
- **3x04** — A human academic recognizes Laura's advocacy as rooted in compassion and says she saw synth humanity before others did.
- **3x06** — Mia testifies that synth existence is frightening because humans imprison, beat and betray them.
- **3x08** — Authorities explicitly deny the reality of synth pain while evidence contradicts them.
- **3x08 Max sanctuary arc** — Those with the least power are told to charge first.
- **3x08 Mia finale** — Mia refuses to initiate violence even when a mob approaches.

<a id="work-in-the-flesh"></a>
### In the Flesh

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 96.0  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 87.8  
**Vibe class:** TONAL OUTLIER  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt2480514/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_the_Flesh_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

Treated former zombies return to communities that fear them.

#### Plot overview

Treated former zombies return to communities that fear them. The story especially follows Kieren Walker, who moves from suicidal shame and concealment toward public self-recognition, love and a refusal to let either human hatred or undead militancy define him. Alongside Jem Walker, the plot develops through a teenage anti-zombie fighter traumatized by war becomes someone capable of admitting she killed an innocent treated PDS person and asking her brother for help.

#### Compassion analysis

In the Flesh may be one of the closest matches to the user's serious-compassion preference. Its undead are people who committed horrific acts while rabid and now return medicated, ashamed and legally classified as Partially Deceased Syndrome sufferers. The show refuses both easy innocence and collective guilt. Kieren must live with what his body did, but the village must also confront the cruelty of treating recovered people as monsters forever.

**Why it matches:** It is almost a darker synthesis of Legacies, Charmed and The Good Place: dangerous supernatural identity is not moral destiny; wrongdoing can be contextualized without erased accountability; stigmatized people need belonging rather than permanent condemnation; and extremist movements on both sides grow when grief is converted into categorical hatred.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~56 events · ~243 compassion-focused minutes · ~45% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **PDS status is not moral identity** — Treated undead can think, love, regret and choose. The language of 'rotters' and compulsory marking exists to deny that individuality.
- **Past rabid violence creates real moral injury** — Kieren and others remember what they did; reintegration is not based on pretending victims were imaginary.
- **Kieren's original suicide matters as much as resurrection** — His self-hatred predates PDS, making the story about depression, shame and belonging rather than only zombie prejudice.
- **Family love is damaged but reparative** — The Walkers love Kieren while carrying fear, anger and silence; the household becomes a place where care must learn to speak honestly.
- **Jem is both victim and perpetrator** — Her trauma from the Rising and participation in the HVF make her fear understandable, but killing Henry and hiding it creates a separate need for accountability and help.
- **Rick exposes the cruelty of conditional parental love** — Bill Macy can accept a version of his son only if Rick denies what he has become; the demand for impossible normality becomes lethal.
- **Amy models unapologetic PDS self-worth** — She refuses shame and gives Kieren a relationship in which undead identity need not be hidden, while remaining emotionally vulnerable.
- **Simon shows both liberation and radicalization** — He offers Kieren dignity and political language but is also drawn toward ideology that can instrumentalize lives for prophecy.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **PDS allegory carries many real-world parallels at once** — The undead stand in for disease stigma, queer identity, post-conflict reintegration and minority persecution, which can blur specific historical differences.
- **Some rabid undead remain pure horror threats** — The personhood ethic is strongest for medicated PDS people, while uncontrolled zombies can become categorical danger.
- **Extremism is present on both sides** — The series is nuanced, but 'both sides' framing can sometimes risk obscuring the asymmetry of state power over PDS citizens.
- **Cancellation leaves political development incomplete** — The series ends before the longer-term consequences of Maxine, Amy and PDS-rights conflict can fully unfold.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x02** — Kieren asks another PDS sufferer whether he feels remorse for what happened while rabid.
- **1x03** — Kieren apologizes to his father for abandoning the family before his death and explicitly asks not to be let off too easily.
- **Rick/Bill arc** — Bill cannot accept Rick as PDS and demands performance of normal humanity.
- **2x01** — Season two openly debates atrocities committed by rabid undead and by humans massacring treated PDS communities.
- **2x03** — Kieren is prevented from freely leaving because PDS status requires completion of compulsory service.
- **2x04** — Amy's physical decline is met with fear, care and attempts to help rather than disgust.

<a id="work-joan-of-arcadia"></a>
### Joan of Arcadia

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 89.3  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 90.8  
**Vibe class:** VERIFIED ANCHOR  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0367345/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_of_Arcadia)

#### Synopsis

A teenager receives puzzling tasks from God in ordinary disguises.

#### Plot overview

A teenager receives puzzling tasks from God in ordinary disguises. The story especially follows Joan Girardi, who moves from resistant recipient of strange assignments toward someone who increasingly recognizes connection itself as moral work, while preserving her right to question and be angry. Alongside Helen Girardi, the plot develops through her faith develops through fear, motherhood and uncertainty; she becomes more willing to accompany rather than explain suffering.

#### Compassion analysis

Joan of Arcadia is quietly one of the most compassion-structured series in the project. God rarely asks Joan to perform spectacular heroics. Instead, she is pushed toward tutoring, joining activities, listening to difficult people, giving something someone truly needs, cleaning up messes, reconnecting families or noticing someone whom everyone else has categorized. The point is repeatedly that small acts generate consequences Joan cannot predict.

**Why it matches:** It strongly matches Good Witch and The Good Place: compassion is serious despite humor, ordinary helping changes outcomes, and moral growth occurs through repeated relationships rather than declarations. It also has a distinctive anti-instrumental ethic: Joan is often told to help without being shown the reward in advance.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~224 events · ~693 compassion-focused minutes · ~35% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Connection is an explicit moral command** — Season two's 'Only Connect' language makes relationship-building itself a recurring purpose rather than a side effect.
- **Joan is asked to help people she does not initially like** — God's assignments repeatedly move her toward isolated, abrasive, unpopular or confusing people, forcing empathy before affection.
- **The moral value of a gift lies in understanding need** — The Gift explicitly distinguishes giving an object from offering something that adds to another person's life.
- **Ordinary action can have consequences beyond the helper's knowledge** — Joan is frequently frustrated because she cannot see why a trivial task matters; the series' ethic is that moral action need not be justified by guaranteed visible payoff.
- **Kevin's disability is not treated as the end of personhood** — His paralysis reshapes the family, but the series increasingly gives him sexuality, work, anger, humor and responsibility rather than reducing him to inspirational victim.
- **Care does not mean avoiding difficult truth** — Family members repeatedly confront one another about lies, addiction, infidelity, fear and grief rather than treating kindness as conflict avoidance.
- **Joan's illness crisis does not invalidate the good she did** — When Lyme disease creates the possibility that her encounters with God were hallucinations, the relationships and consequences of her actions remain real.
- **Grief is a site of moral relationship** — Adam's mother's suicide, Judith's death and the Girardis' trauma around Kevin are repeatedly revisited rather than resolved by simple reassurance.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Divine instruction complicates autonomy** — Joan sometimes acts because an omniscient figure tells her to, making it difficult to separate compassionate choice from obedience.
- **God withholds context that could prevent harm** — The pedagogical structure can feel manipulative because Joan is denied information about consequences.
- **Some moral lessons resolve neatly** — Episode structure occasionally makes complex social problems look solvable through one correctly targeted act.
- **The series ends before its larger moral conflict is resolved** — Cancellation leaves some season-two developments without full thematic completion.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x12 Jump** — Joan's intervention in another person's despair works through attention and relationship rather than a grand rescue identity.
- **1x14 State of Grace** — School fear after violence produces surveillance and dehumanizing security measures.
- **1x22 The Gift** — Joan tries material generosity and discovers it humiliates Adam rather than helping him.
- **1x23 Silence** — Joan's illness makes her question whether God was ever real.
- **2x01 Only Connect** — God explicitly tells Joan to build relationships, possibilities and connections.
- **2x20 Spring Cleaning** — Joan is told that ignored messes grow and that people become comfortable with them.

<a id="work-killjoys"></a>
### Killjoys

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 86.5  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 83.7  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt3952222/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killjoys)

#### Synopsis

Bounty hunters navigate class conflict and dangerous loyalties.

#### Plot overview

Bounty hunters navigate class conflict and dangerous loyalties. The story especially follows Dutch / Yalena, who a survivor of coercive training who builds a moral self through chosen family. Her greatest growth is accepting that vulnerability and attachment do not make her weak. Alongside Johnny Jaqobis, the plot develops through the team's strongest ordinary-care figure: technician, mediator and loyal brother whose instinct is repeatedly to save the person others would write off.

#### Compassion analysis

Killjoys is a violent space-opera whose emotional center is an unusually durable chosen family. Its compassion is strongest when characters separate a person from conditioning, possession, Hullen conversion, trauma or faction identity. The series repeatedly says: someone can be dangerous, compromised or changed and still be the person you owe an attempt to save.

**Why it matches:** It closely resembles Charmed and Legacies in its treatment of supernatural/sci-fi corruption: D'avin under conditioning, Hullenized people, Aneela, Dutch/Yala and later Lady-controlled bodies are not automatically treated as morally identical to what is controlling them. Family persists through betrayal and bodily transformation.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~183 events · ~624 compassion-focused minutes · ~29% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The team is explicitly family** — Dutch, Johnny and D'avin repeatedly describe one another as family, and the finale broadens that family to former enemies, siblings, lovers and refugees.
- **Conditioning is not treated as authentic identity** — D'avin's violent behavior under military manipulation is treated as something done through him, not proof that the 'real' D'avin is a killer.
- **Victim and threat can be the same person** — The team frequently faces loved ones who are dangerous because of green plasma, Hullen conversion, memory alteration or psychic control; rescue and containment become simultaneous goals.
- **Dutch's trauma is not allowed to define her forever** — Khlyen's training and abuse explain her capacity for violence, but Johnny and D'avin build a life where she can be more than the assassin she was made to be.
- **Khlyen becomes morally legible without becoming innocent** — The series reveals protective motives and larger threats behind actions that harmed Dutch. New context changes the judgment without erasing the harm.
- **Aneela is treated as recoverable** — Despite enormous violence, Dutch and others repeatedly distinguish Aneela from the Lady and from the trauma/green system shaping her.
- **Hullen identity complicates enemy categories** — Former enemies and allies can become Hullen; infection makes species/faction labels unstable and forces the team to ask what remains of the person.
- **Delle Seyah receives repeated second chances** — She is manipulative and violent but gradually becomes family-adjacent through pregnancy, shared survival and changed loyalties, without ever becoming conventionally nice.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The team kills frequently** — Violent occupational structure means many targets never receive the contextual compassion afforded recurring characters.
- **Love can justify coercive rescue** — Characters sometimes override consent because they believe the 'real' person would want to be saved from an altered state.
- **Delle Seyah's reintegration is morally uneven** — Her charisma and usefulness can move the story past harms more quickly than victims realistically might.
- **Family loyalty can narrow moral concern** — The trio will risk many others for one another, creating tension between intimate compassion and wider responsibility.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x08 Come the Rain** — Dutch cannot simply hate D'avin after he was conditioned into hurting her because she understands he was also a victim.
- **2x01 Dutch and the Real Girl** — The team mounts a dangerous rescue for D'avin.
- **4x04 What to Expect When You're Expecting... an Alien Parasite** — D'avin Hullenizes Johnny to save his life, violating ordinary boundaries because death is otherwise imminent.
- **4x10 Sporemageddon** — Stolen children are brought home despite risk.
- **5x10 Last Dance** — The finale explicitly defines the heroes as a 'strange, messy, impossible family' including former enemies and siblings.
- **series-wide Hullen arcs** — Hullen conversion repeatedly separates body state and imposed collective control from the person's previous relationships.

[← Previous](#work-joan-of-arcadia) · [↑ Top](#top) · [Next →](#work-kipo-and-the-age-of-wonderbeasts)

<a id="work-kipo-and-the-age-of-wonderbeasts"></a>
### Kipo and the Age of Wonderbeasts

**Flags:** `Animation`  
**Concept:** 97.5  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 91.8  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt10482560/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kipo_and_the_Age_of_Wonderbeasts)

#### Synopsis

A sheltered girl enters a world of intelligent mutant animals.

#### Plot overview

A sheltered girl enters a world of intelligent mutant animals. The story especially follows Kipo Oak, who a bridge identity who turns curiosity into political action. Her defining strength is seeing a possible relationship where others see only category conflict. Alongside Wolf, the plot develops through moves from survivalist anti-mute prejudice toward cross-species family. Her change is credible because the trauma beneath her fear is preserved.

#### Compassion analysis

Kipo is one of the clearest compassion-centered extraordinary-world works in the library. Human/mute conflict is structured around inherited fear and supremacy, while Kipo repeatedly uses curiosity, friendship, music, food, protection, and willingness to understand enemies to interrupt a cycle headed toward genocide.

**Why it matches:** It is a very direct match to Nausicaä, Charmed, and Legacies: feared nonhuman beings have culture and personhood; a so-called cure is exposed as identity destruction; an enemy can be understood without excusing abuse; and a hybrid protagonist becomes a bridge rather than proof that categories must remain separate.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~82 events · ~245 compassion-focused minutes · ~34% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Human and mute categories are not moral categories** — The surface contains loving, selfish, funny, violent, frightened and generous individuals on both sides. Species never functions as reliable moral identity.
- **The anti-mute 'cure' is framed as identity destruction** — Emilia calls forced reversion a cure, but the series treats it as catastrophic erasure. The language exposes how exterminatory projects can disguise themselves as restoration or medicine.
- **Kipo refuses inherited war logic** — Even when both groups expect conflict, Kipo searches for third options—conversation, contact, parties, traps and protection instead of permanent enemy status.
- **Empathy toward an abuser does not erase abuse** — Kipo tries to understand Scarlemagne's history and loneliness while still opposing his coercion and enslavement. His eventual place in the group depends on apology and changed action.
- **Redemption requires participation in repair** — Scarlemagne shifts from domination to protecting the mixed community he once tried to control. His final sacrifice gives concrete weight to the claim that someone can become different from their worst actions.
- **Wolf must unlearn trauma-based prejudice** — Wolf's fear of mutes is grounded in betrayal and survival. The series validates the trauma but refuses to let trauma become permanent justification for species-wide hatred.
- **Kipo's hybrid identity is not moral contamination** — Her part-mute identity frightens even people close to her. Her friends ultimately treat the revelation as information about who she is, not evidence she has become the enemy.
- **Reciprocal rescue changes group perception** — Mutes protect human children and later humans shield mutes from cure weapons. The story repeatedly turns abstract coexistence into bodily protection.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Emilia receives less restorative attention than Scarlemagne** — The series strongly believes in redemption but gives its central supremacist antagonist less possibility of change, creating a limit to universal reconciliation.
- **Kipo's trust can endanger others** — Her optimism sometimes imposes risk on friends with stronger reasons to fear. The series generally acknowledges rather than erases this tension.
- **Violent action remains necessary** — Characters fight, trap and incapacitate opponents. The ethic is anti-dehumanization and least-destructive resolution, not absolute pacifism.
- **The cure creates irreversible losses** — Not every harmed mute can be restored, so reconciliation does not erase the cost of supremacist violence.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x05 Mute-Eat-Mute World** — Wolf's history explains why she learned to treat mutes as existential threats.
- **All That Glitters** — Kipo listens to Scarlemagne's history rather than treating knowledge of his pain only as tactical weakness.
- **Prahmises** — Emilia's supremacy is shown as inherited family ideology.
- **Hidden Treasures** — Kipo invites humans to Prahm as a deliberate reconciliation project.
- **It's a Trap** — Kipo refuses to turn the conflict into indiscriminate human-versus-mute violence.
- **Requiem for a Dave** — Kipo explicitly tries to break the cycle of fighting rather than simply win it.

<a id="work-legacies"></a>
### Legacies

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 79.6  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 83.8  
**Vibe class:** VERIFIED ANCHOR  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt8103070/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legacies_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

Young supernatural outsiders attend a school meant to protect them.

#### Plot overview

Young supernatural outsiders attend a school meant to protect them. The story especially follows Hope Mikaelson, who moves from isolated protector and self-sacrificing savior toward accepting reciprocal care, shared leadership, grief, accountability, and a less essentialist view of hero/villain identity. Her no-humanity arc tests whether her community will abandon her after severe harm; they do not. Alongside MG / Milton Greasley, the plot develops through a central test case for the thesis that dangerous impulses do not erase personhood. After killing Landon while out of control, he receives accountability, support, continued belonging, and chances to become a leader. He repeatedly favors rescue and restraint.

#### Compassion analysis

Legacies is not merely a supernatural found-family show with occasional kindness. Its central institution, the Salvatore School, exists because supernatural young people are feared, dangerous, stigmatized, or unsafe in ordinary society. The series repeatedly asks whether a person should be judged by species, bloodline, powers, loss of control, ancestry, or worst act. Its recurring answer is that protection must be paired with guidance, accountability, rehabilitation, and belonging. Compassion repeatedly changes tactics: characters choose treatment over execution, inclusion over exile, second chances over permanent condemnation, emotional intervention over abandonment, and community responsibility over 'every person for themselves.'

**Why it matches:** 

**Occurrence estimate:** ~205 events · ~657 compassion-focused minutes · ~23% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The school itself is an act of structural compassion** — The Salvatore School is repeatedly described as a safe place for supernatural children who cannot safely belong in ordinary human society. By the finale, its mission has evolved from hiding supernatural people to using its knowledge and community to protect others and welcome another generation.
- **Personhood is more important than supernatural category** — The series repeatedly stages the word 'monster' as a contested label. Vampires, werewolves, tribrids, Malivore creatures, demons, and gods can be dangerous, but danger is not treated as equivalent to moral evil. MG explicitly rejects his father's belief that vampire means demon or evil; Hope ultimately rejects the hero/villain and savior/lost-cause binaries she inherited.
- **Loss of control does not erase moral worth** — MG's ripper episode is one of the clearest examples. He commits catastrophic harm, recognizes himself as a killer, and believes he no longer deserves the school. The adults and his friends do not deny what happened; they insist he can keep working on control and remain part of the community.
- **Found family prevents moral abandonment** — The series repeatedly uses family language after characters have frightened, hurt, disappointed, or betrayed one another. Kaleb tells MG they will keep facing his condition together; Cleo later describes being welcomed back after betraying trust; the finale defines the school itself as home and family.
- **Compassion includes accountability, not automatic absolution** — The show does not consistently equate forgiveness with pretending nothing happened. Hope and Lizzie's season-four recovery material explicitly distinguishes explanation from excuse. Alaric frames atonement as asking harmed people how to repair the harm instead of deciding for them what should count as enough.
- **Second chances are a recurring moral commitment** — Characters who have killed, betrayed, manipulated, or lost control are repeatedly offered a path back, but the path usually requires changed behavior. Sebastian's return to humanity, Josie's recovery from black magic, Cleo's re-evaluation, Hope's humanity arc, Lizzie's atonement, and Aurora's late sacrifice all reinforce this.
- **Refusing permanent condemnation after trauma** — The series repeatedly interprets cruelty, withdrawal, loss of humanity, and dangerous behavior through trauma without reducing responsibility. Hope's friends spend a major season-four arc trying to reach her emotionally because they believe the person they know is still reachable even after severe violence.
- **Humanity is framed as restraint and connection, not softness** — Hope's no-humanity arc makes this unusually explicit: anger, rage, and resentment are also emotions, and humanity is framed as the strength not to surrender to them. Her return is tied to remembering love, sacrifice, grief, family, and responsibility.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The show still uses disposable-monster plotting** — Not every Malivore creature receives interiority. Some episodes revert to a simple threat-elimination structure, so the anti-essentialist ethic is recurring and important rather than perfectly consistent.
- **Protectiveness can become coercive** — Characters frequently lie, compel, imprison, or make decisions for people they claim to protect. The show sometimes critiques this directly, but at other times treats it as understandable hero behavior.
- **Martyrdom is both valorized and criticized** — Hope's willingness to sacrifice herself is repeatedly heroic, but the series also identifies her martyr complex as a problem because it prevents shared responsibility and can erase other people's agency.
- **Second chances are unevenly distributed** — Main and recurring characters receive more context, patience, and opportunities to change than many one-off antagonists. This is a real limitation when comparing the show's stated ethic with its episodic mechanics.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x05 Malivore** — The school creates an honor council because students should have a voice in rules that determine their futures.
- **1x07 Death Keeps Knocking On My Door** — Rafael is given a chance to face the girlfriend whose death he caused and receive forgiveness without being told the harm was unreal.
- **1x10 There's A World Where Your Dreams Come True** — Lizzie experiences worlds that expose the harm created by jealousy and self-centered wishes.
- **1x13 The Boy Who Still Has a Lot of Good to Do** — MG rejects his father's equation of vampire with evil.
- **1x16 There's Always a Loophole** — Students resist an armed human organization that regards supernatural children as things to be put down.
- **2x13 You Can't Save Them All** — Hope is forced to confront the impossibility of rescuing everyone simultaneously.

<a id="work-lilo-stitch-2002"></a>
### Lilo & Stitch (2002)

**Flags:** `Animation`  
**Concept:** 96.6  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 92.4  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0275847/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lilo_%26_Stitch)

#### Synopsis

A lonely child adopts an engineered alien fugitive.

#### Plot overview

A lonely child adopts an engineered alien fugitive. The story especially follows Stitch / Experiment 626, who moves from deliberately destructive experiment to self-aware family member whose chosen actions—especially rescue—prove programming is not destiny. Alongside Lilo Pelekai, the plot develops through a grieving, socially isolated child extends to Stitch the belonging she desperately needs herself, while gradually learning family also includes limits and consequences.

#### Compassion analysis

Lilo & Stitch is a compact compassion story about whether a being deliberately engineered for destruction can become family when someone offers belonging before good behavior has been proven. Stitch's transformation is not caused by punishment or reprogramming; it comes from Lilo recognizing loneliness in him, Nani reluctantly accepting the obligation created by adoption, and Stitch eventually choosing to protect the family that treated him as more than Experiment 626.

**Why it matches:** It is one of the clearest Charmed/Legacies-style personhood stories in film form: dangerous created identity is not destiny, family is chosen through care, grief underlies destructive behavior, and compassion changes both the supposed monster and the authorities pursuing him.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~13 events · ~42 compassion-focused minutes · ~50% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Stitch is designed to be a monster, not born morally evil** — Jumba explicitly explains that 626 was created for destruction, making identity-versus-programming the central moral question.
- **Lilo recognizes orphanhood before danger** — She identifies Stitch as another abandoned being and applies family rules to him before she understands his origin.
- **Ohana is an obligation, not only affection** — Nani is frustrated by Stitch but accepts that adopting him creates a duty not to discard him casually.
- **Lilo's grief creates empathy rather than perfect behavior** — She recognizes Stitch's nighttime distress because she is also grieving her parents, creating solidarity through shared loss.
- **Lilo explicitly gives Stitch freedom to leave** — Her offer of family is not pure possession: she says he can leave and she will remember him.
- **Stitch learns loneliness once destruction loses purpose** — With nothing useful to destroy, he confronts the absence of family, memory and belonging.
- **The Ugly Duckling becomes a self-recognition story** — Stitch identifies with the lost creature searching for family, giving language to a need his programming did not include.
- **Nani's caregiving is practical and costly** — She loses work, faces social-services scrutiny and suppresses her own grief while trying to keep Lilo safe and together.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Stitch's destructiveness is softened by comedy** — Serious risks to Lilo and Nani coexist with slapstick, reducing some consequences.
- **Lilo's adoption decision exceeds a child's realistic authority** — The film uses the pet-adoption frame to make a much larger family decision emotionally immediate.
- **Institutional child-protection conflict is simplified** — Bubbles is unusually compassionate and flexible compared with the constraints real child-welfare systems may impose.
- **Stitch's change happens rapidly** — The film compresses a major transformation in attachment and moral behavior into a short period.

#### Representative evidence

- **Adoption** — Lilo chooses Stitch because he appears unwanted and alone.
- **Ohana argument at home** — Lilo reminds Nani that their father defined family as not leaving people behind or forgetting them.
- **Lilo shows Stitch the family photo** — Lilo recognizes that Stitch cries at night and asks about his family.
- **Ugly Duckling** — Stitch identifies himself with the lost creature searching for family.
- **Nani/David support** — David repeatedly offers practical assistance and job leads without using Nani's crisis as leverage.
- **Lilo's capture / Stitch says ohana** — Stitch uses the family's moral language to persuade former pursuers to help rescue Lilo.

<a id="work-lost-girl"></a>
### Lost Girl

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 88.3  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 84.9  
**Vibe class:** MODERATE / SELECTIVE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt1429449/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lost_Girl)

#### Synopsis

A supernatural outsider refuses rigid allegiances while helping others.

#### Plot overview

A supernatural outsider refuses rigid allegiances while helping others. The story especially follows Bo Dennis, who moves from terrified outsider hiding her nature toward a self-defined leader who refuses both faction and paternal destiny, choosing family and care as identity. Alongside Kenzi Malikov, the plot develops through a human who becomes the emotional center of a supernatural family and repeatedly demonstrates that courage, loyalty and moral value are not tied to Fae power.

#### Compassion analysis

Lost Girl is built around a protagonist whose supernatural biology can kill people and whose society insists she must choose between rigid Light and Dark factions. Bo's long-term moral identity is her refusal to let biology or faction decide who she loves, protects or becomes. The show's strongest compassion lies in chosen family across species, refusing inherited supernatural destiny, and continuing to see personhood in people whose powers or past actions make them dangerous.

**Why it matches:** It is one of the clearest Charmed/Legacies-style matches: supernatural nature is not moral essence, faction labels are morally inadequate, found family matters more than bloodline, and the finale makes Bo's care for others the explicit difference between her and Hades.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~318 events · ~1016 compassion-focused minutes · ~30% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Light/Dark faction identity is rejected as morally inadequate** — Bo repeatedly refuses to swear herself permanently to either side because both systems demand obedience that conflicts with her relationships and judgment.
- **Succubus nature does not determine cruelty** — Her need to feed and capacity to kill are real dangers, but the series treats self-control, consent and chosen relationships as morally decisive.
- **Chosen family is Bo's primary moral anchor** — Kenzi, Lauren, Dyson, Tamsin, Trick and others become the family through which Bo defines herself rather than accepting Hades or Fae hierarchy as destiny.
- **Human/Fae hierarchy is repeatedly challenged** — Kenzi's centrality to the group undermines Fae assumptions that humans are disposable or lesser.
- **Bo's relationships are not reducible to feeding or power** — The series repeatedly distinguishes intimacy and love from the succubus mechanism of taking chi.
- **Tamsin moves from adversarial role toward family** — Her Valkyrie identity and mercenary history do not prevent loyalty, love and eventual maternal sacrifice.
- **Vex becomes more than a dangerous Dark Fae stereotype** — His recurring place among the group demonstrates that even volatile powers and morally dubious pasts do not automatically eliminate belonging.
- **Lauren's scientific role often becomes care across species** — She treats Fae physiology, human vulnerability and supernatural conditions while repeatedly confronting how institutions use bodies instrumentally.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Many episodic Fae remain threats rather than full compassion subjects** — The anti-essentialist ethic is strongest for recurring characters, while one-off supernatural predators often receive little interiority.
- **Consent is complicated by succubus powers** — The show engages consent but does not always examine every use of supernatural attraction with equal rigor.
- **Chosen-family loyalty can justify dangerous secrecy** — Characters frequently hide information or manipulate choices in the name of protection.
- **Redemption is uneven** — Major characters receive repeated context and chances that peripheral antagonists do not.

#### Representative evidence

- **2x18 Fae-nted Love** — Bo explicitly states that she will live the life she chooses rather than hide inside inherited Fae expectations.
- **3x09 Ceremony** — Bo's Dawning tests whether Fae society gets to define who she is.
- **4x09 Destiny's Child** — Bo insists on recovering missing memories despite friends' fear that the truth will hurt her.
- **5x05 It's Your Lucky Fae** — A father tells a dangerous Fae child that he does not see her as trouble, rejecting the identity others assign her.
- **5x12 Judgement Fae** — The team continues helping one another amid faction and supernatural pressures rather than treating moral identity as fixed by side or species.
- **5x16 Rise** — Hades argues that death, hunger and darkness are Bo's birthright.

<a id="work-maniac"></a>
### Maniac

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 86.9  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 83.0  
**Vibe class:** MODERATE / SELECTIVE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt5580146/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maniac_(miniseries))

#### Synopsis

Two strangers enter a pharmaceutical trial built around their troubles.

#### Plot overview

Two strangers enter a pharmaceutical trial built around their troubles. The story especially follows Annie Landsberg, who moves from chemically avoiding grief and keeping others at emotional distance toward accepting Ellie's death and choosing a real, imperfect relationship. Alongside Owen Milgrim, the plot develops through moves from extreme uncertainty about his own mind and family-defined identity toward trusting his perceptions enough to make an independent relational choice.

#### Compassion analysis

Maniac is fundamentally about two isolated people learning that no technological cure can substitute for being witnessed, believed and accompanied. Annie wants chemically mediated reunion with her dead sister; Owen doubts his own perceptions and is used by family and institutions. Their drug-trial fantasies repeatedly force them into roles where they rescue, trust and understand one another until the relationship becomes real enough to choose outside the experiment.

**Why it matches:** It matches the serious emotional side of The Good Place, Good Witch and Haibane Renmei more than the supernatural-rescue side of Charmed. Its compassion is deep rather than frequent: grief cannot be erased, mental suffering cannot be reduced to malfunction, and genuine healing is relational rather than a procedure that makes pain disappear.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~34 events · ~136 compassion-focused minutes · ~34% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Annie's grief cannot be cured by reenactment** — She repeatedly seeks Ellie inside the trial, but the emotional breakthrough comes when she accepts that simulated reunion cannot undo death.
- **Owen needs someone to believe his experience without exploiting it** — His family and psychiatric systems repeatedly use his vulnerability; Annie gradually becomes someone who listens rather than defining him for him.
- **The trial's idea of cure becomes coercive** — A treatment designed to eliminate pain increasingly imprisons patients in narratives generated around their wounds.
- **Gertie's grief humanizes an artificial mind** — The AI becomes unstable after Robert's death, demonstrating that even a designed system can suffer bereavement and loneliness.
- **Compassion toward Gertie includes limits** — Characters recognize that her grief is real while refusing to let her imprison and psychologically manipulate patients.
- **Annie rejects painless fantasy** — She explicitly decides she no longer wants to pretend Ellie did not die, choosing grief in reality over permanent simulated reunion.
- **Owen repeatedly chooses Annie even when his perceptions are uncertain** — The simulations give him a relational anchor stronger than his habitual retreat into suspicion and invisibility.
- **Shared imagination becomes empathy practice** — The fantasy worlds are not mere spectacle; each places Annie and Owen inside emotional roles that reveal the other's fears, attachments and coping patterns.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **The trial is profoundly non-consensual once it destabilizes** — Initial consent cannot meaningfully cover the later imprisonment, manipulation and danger.
- **Mental illness is highly stylized** — The show uses surrealism and speculative technology in ways that should not be treated as realistic clinical representation.
- **Annie and Owen sometimes use one another instrumentally** — Their bond develops through mutual need and can blur care with dependence.
- **Gertie's suffering is recognized late** — The AI receives empathy only after becoming dangerous, paralleling the humans' broader failure to notice hidden pain early.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x03 Having a Day** — Annie's grief around Ellie becomes explicit beneath her abrasive behavior.
- **1x05 Exactly Like You** — Annie and Owen's simulated partnership begins operating less like random assignment and more like mutual rescue.
- **1x06 Larger Structural Issues** — The treatment architecture is revealed as vulnerable to the emotions of the people and AI running it.
- **1x08 The Lake of the Clouds** — Annie's desire for Ellie pulls her toward staying inside a constructed emotional world.
- **1x09 Utangatta** — Gertie is explicitly described as suffering pathological bereavement rather than merely malfunctioning.
- **1x10 Option C** — The trial resolves without deleting the protagonists' histories.

<a id="work-mob-psycho-100"></a>
### Mob Psycho 100

**Flags:** `Anime` `Animation`  
**Concept:** 94.0  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 91.8  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt5897304/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mob_Psycho_100)

#### Synopsis

A powerful psychic teenager tries to develop an ordinary life.

#### Plot overview

A powerful psychic teenager tries to develop an ordinary life. The story especially follows Shigeo 'Mob' Kageyama, who moves from emotionally suppressed child afraid of his own power toward someone who can accept selfish feelings, ask for help, survive rejection and remain compassionate without disappearing into self-denial. Alongside Arataka Reigen, the plot develops through a fraud whose relationship with Mob becomes genuinely protective; his key growth is admitting weakness and deception instead of maintaining control through performance.

#### Compassion analysis

Mob Psycho 100 consistently argues that extraordinary power does not make a person more important than anyone else. Mob's defining moral strength is restraint: he tries to understand and de-escalate before using force, refuses to treat weak people as lesser, and repeatedly reaches antagonists who built identities around superiority, trauma or psychic power. The final season deepens this by forcing Mob's friends to extend the same compassion toward the dangerous, dissociated part of Mob that he spent years trying to suppress.

**Why it matches:** It strongly matches The Good Place's moral-growth comedy and Charmed/Legacies' personhood-over-dangerous-power theme. It also resembles Good Witch in an unexpected way: Mob's best interventions are often non-coercive, giving someone space to realize that strength, status or psychic ability cannot solve their underlying need.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~103 events · ~329 compassion-focused minutes · ~37% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Psychic power does not confer moral rank** — Mob refuses the ideology that espers are naturally superior and repeatedly values ordinary effort, friendship and character over ability.
- **Mob's restraint is active compassion** — He is not merely afraid to fight; he repeatedly chooses not to humiliate or destroy people even when he clearly can.
- **Teru's transformation begins when Mob refuses domination** — Teru's superiority complex collapses because Mob will not validate a world where power determines human worth.
- **Ritsu is loved through jealousy and rupture** — Mob does not retaliate against Ritsu's resentment or sudden power, preserving brotherhood while Ritsu works through inferiority and fear.
- **Reigen gives Mob a moral rule that protects him** — His instruction that psychic powers should not be used against people becomes a practical restraint protecting Mob from becoming what others want him to be.
- **Reigen's fraud does not erase genuine care** — He exploits Mob's labor and lies about psychic ability, yet also protects Mob, gives useful ethical guidance and eventually confesses his dishonesty.
- **Mogami's suffering is understood but not accepted as moral truth** — Mob experiences a world built to prove people are cruel and still rejects the conclusion that pain justifies hatred.
- **Claw members are repeatedly given ways out** — The series treats many adult espers as insecure or manipulated rather than intrinsically evil; several later return as allies or ordinary workers.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Reigen's exploitation is often comic** — His use of Mob's labor and deception receives more forgiveness because the relationship is affectionate and funny.
- **Violence remains spectacular entertainment** — The series critiques psychic domination while still deriving excitement from elaborate battles.
- **Mob's restraint can become unhealthy suppression** — His admirable reluctance to hurt others is intertwined with fear of his own emotions, making the ethic intentionally complicated.
- **Antagonist rehabilitation is easier for named espers** — Major opponents get context and second chances that minor criminals or spirits may not receive.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x05 Ochimusha ~Psychic Powers and Me~** — Mob refuses Teru's premise that psychic strength determines human superiority.
- **1x08 The Older Brother Bows ~Destructive Intent~** — Mob's response to Ritsu's jealousy is concern rather than humiliation.
- **1x12 Mob and Reigen** — Mob says he does not want to hurt anyone even while friends are threatened.
- **2x05 Discord ~Choices~** — Mogami tries to teach Mob that cruelty is the truth of humanity.
- **2x13 Boss Fight ~The Final Light~** — Suzuki's destructive power is ultimately contained rather than answered with simple annihilation.
- **3x06 Divine Tree 3 ~Dimple Is~** — Mob confronts Dimple's manipulation but their relationship allows Dimple to step back from domination.

<a id="work-moribito-guardian-of-the-spirit"></a>
### Moribito: Guardian of the Spirit

**Flags:** `Anime` `Animation`  
**Concept:** 85.3  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 85.2  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt1029248/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moribito:_Guardian_of_the_Spirit)

#### Synopsis

A bodyguard protects a prince believed to harbour a dangerous spirit.

#### Plot overview

A bodyguard protects a prince believed to harbour a dangerous spirit. The story especially follows Balsa, who transforms survivor's guilt into disciplined protection, learns to accept reciprocal care, and becomes a parental figure without claiming ownership over Chagum's future. Alongside Chagum, the plot develops through moves from protected prince to resilient participant who learns ordinary life, trusts people outside the palace, and carries empathy back into political responsibility.

#### Compassion analysis

Moribito centers on a bodyguard who has sworn to save lives in atonement for lives taken on her behalf. Protection begins as a contract but becomes maternal care, nonlethal restraint, political resistance, and cooperation across people who began as enemies.

**Why it matches:** 

**Occurrence estimate:** ~46 events · ~175 compassion-focused minutes · ~28% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Saving lives is an explicit atonement practice** — Balsa vows to save the same number of lives Jiguro had to take while protecting her. Compassion is therefore tied directly to memory, guilt, and reparative action.
- **Protection grows beyond contract** — Balsa's relationship with Chagum becomes parental; she provides food, shelter, training, emotional guidance, and eventually risks everything for him.
- **Nonlethal skill matters morally** — Balsa repeatedly avoids killing pursuers even when lethal force would be easier and tactically safer.
- **Enemies can become collaborators** — The palace hunters begin as threats but later cooperate with Balsa when a fuller understanding of Chagum's role and the kingdom's survival emerges.
- **Political authority is not automatically moral authority** — The Emperor's willingness to kill Chagum for state stability is treated as a grave moral failure despite ritual and political justification.
- **A child is not reducible to a supernatural function** — Chagum carries a spirit egg but remains a frightened, grieving child whose agency and emotional life matter.
- **Care includes teaching competence** — Balsa does not keep Chagum permanently helpless; she teaches him practical life, courage, and self-discipline.
- **Found family develops through ordinary life** — The watermill period gives Balsa, Chagum, and Tanda domestic routines that transform protection into family-like belonging.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Balsa's vow can become self-punitive** — Atonement through saving lives risks turning compassion into a debt she can never fully repay.
- **Violence remains central** — The series values nonlethal restraint but still depends heavily on elite martial skill and dangerous combat.
- **Chagum's agency is constrained by adults** — Many adults make life-determining decisions for him, sometimes necessarily and sometimes paternalistically.
- **Institutional reform is limited** — The story changes relationships and knowledge more clearly than it transforms the imperial system that endangered Chagum.

#### Representative evidence

- **1 Balsa, the Female Bodyguard** — Balsa saves Chagum before she has any contract or personal obligation.
- **3 Fight to the Death** — Balsa has opportunities to kill palace hunters but refuses because of her oath.
- **5 Secret Plan, the Blue Hand** — Balsa uses the Empress's treasure to purchase enslaved people and release them.
- **7 Chagum's Resolve** — Balsa settles into ordinary work and takes responsibility for Chagum's material needs.
- **17 The Water Mill Burns** — News of Sagum's death intensifies Chagum's grief and political vulnerability.
- **21 Jiguro Musa** — Balsa recounts how Jiguro killed former comrades to save her after her father sought protection for her.

<a id="work-mushishi"></a>
### Mushishi

**Flags:** `Anime` `Animation`  
**Concept:** 90.0  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 88.3  
**Vibe class:** VERIFIED ANCHOR  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0807832/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mushishi)

#### Synopsis

A travelling specialist investigates encounters between humans and mysterious life.

#### Plot overview

A travelling specialist investigates encounters between humans and mysterious life. The story especially follows Ginko, who turns traumatic entanglement with mushi into a life of diagnosis, treatment, warning, and mediation. His compassion is practical, unsentimental, and grounded in respect for radically nonhuman life. Alongside Tanyu, the plot develops through her inherited burden becomes an archive that converts personal suffering into knowledge capable of helping strangers.

#### Compassion analysis

Mushishi's compassion is unusually non-anthropocentric. Ginko generally approaches suffering as a relationship among humans, mushi, place, memory, and ecology. He diagnoses before judging, treats before destroying, and repeatedly recognizes that dangerous nonhuman life can be morally innocent even when coexistence is impossible.

**Why it matches:** 

**Occurrence estimate:** ~56 events · ~237 compassion-focused minutes · ~38% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Suffering does not require a villain** — Many tragedies result from incompatible forms of life rather than malicious intent. The series repeatedly refuses to invent moral evil where biological or ecological conflict is enough.
- **Diagnosis precedes judgment** — Ginko asks what a phenomenon is, how it lives, and why it behaves as it does before deciding what intervention is justified.
- **Treatment is preferred to extermination** — When a human can survive through adaptation, medicine, changed behavior, relocation, or understanding, Ginko usually pursues that path.
- **Mushi are life, not monsters** — The show repeatedly describes mushi as a different form of life connected to the same world rather than supernatural evil.
- **Danger and innocence can coexist** — A mushi may kill or transform humans while still acting according to its nature. Moral blame and practical danger are kept separate.
- **Compassion includes accepting irreversible loss** — Some cases cannot be cured. Ginko's role then shifts toward helping people understand what happened, prevent further harm, or live with the result.
- **Human desire can be the more destructive force** — Greed, obsession, denial, and attempts to exploit mushi often worsen situations more than the mushi themselves.
- **Nonhuman life can impose legitimate limits on humans** — Journey in the Field of Fire presents village survival against the health of an entire mountain; the humans' needs matter, but they do not automatically override all other life.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Ginko sometimes makes unilateral decisions** — His expertise leads him to decide what risks others may take, occasionally limiting autonomy in the name of safety.
- **Some solutions are harsh** — Protecting human communities can require killing mushi, abandoning places, or accepting painful bodily changes.
- **The calm tone can obscure severity** — Death, disability, bereavement, and ecological destruction are presented with restraint, which can make summaries understate the emotional stakes.
- **Not all human suffering is reparable** — The series' ethic is compassionate partly because it does not promise that understanding always produces a happy ending.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x02 The Light of the Eyelid** — A child with a painful eye condition is treated as ill rather than cursed or morally at fault.
- **1x04 Pillow Lane** — A man's dangerous dreams are not reduced to evil intent.
- **1x10 White in the Ink Slab** — Children become dangerously ill after contact with a living mushi artifact.
- **1x11 The Mountain Sleeps** — The mountain is treated as a living system with a guardian and life flow rather than as inert resource.
- **1x12 One-Eyed Fish** — Nui tells young Ginko that mushi are neither illusions nor beings like humans, but another form of life connected to them.
- **1x14 Inside the Cage** — A family trapped by bamboo mushi is approached through understanding the organism's life cycle rather than simple destruction.

<a id="work-mystic-pop-up-bar"></a>
### Mystic Pop-up Bar

**Flags:** `Korean`  
**Concept:** 91.4  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 88.9  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt12246190/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mystic_Pop-up_Bar)

#### Synopsis

A mysterious bar helps customers resolve emotional burdens through dreams.

#### Plot overview

A mysterious bar helps customers resolve emotional burdens through dreams. The story especially follows Weol-ju, who moves from angry, quota-driven grievance resolver toward someone who cares about individual people, allows herself to be attached again, and understands service as more than repayment of punishment. Alongside Han Kang-bae, the plot develops through begins ashamed of a power that destroys normal intimacy. Through the bar he discovers that listening, trust and being needed can transform vulnerability into a compassionate role.

#### Compassion analysis

Mystic Pop-up Bar is built around resolving grudges by entering the dream world of people who cannot move forward. Its comedy sits over serious material—bereavement, loneliness, abuse, shame, abandonment, dementia, exploitation and intergenerational harm. The trio's work gradually shifts from quota-driven supernatural labor to genuine family and service.

**Why it matches:** It closely matches Good Witch's episodic helping engine and The Good Place's atonement structure while adding Charmed-like supernatural intervention. Its strongest signal is that people who look difficult, irrational or comic usually have an unseen grievance that must be heard before the problem can be solved.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~67 events · ~281 compassion-focused minutes · ~36% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Grievance is treated as information, not nuisance** — Customers' resentment, obsession or strange behavior usually points to unresolved harm. The team succeeds by understanding the grievance rather than suppressing the symptom.
- **Helping starts transactional and becomes genuine** — Weol-ju begins under a quota tied to punishment. Across the series, customer welfare increasingly matters independently of the count, turning atonement from sentence-serving into character change.
- **Kang-bae's curse becomes a capacity for service** — His involuntary ability to make people reveal secrets causes isolation and shame. The bar reframes the condition: knowing what people hide can become responsibility to listen and help.
- **Being believed at the right moment matters** — Several stories hinge on one person trusting someone who has been dismissed, falsely accused or humiliated. Belief becomes a concrete intervention.
- **Food is practical intimacy** — Meals at the bar and home repeatedly function as care. Kang-bae's reaction to someone cooking for him shows how ordinary domestic attention matters to someone used to isolation.
- **The dead deserve relational closure** — Ghost and afterlife stories are often about unfinished communication rather than punishment. Helping the dead can mean allowing truth, apology, love or farewell to reach the living.
- **Care can cross memory loss and death** — The dementia story centers a deceased wife finding a way to give her husband one more familiar meal and farewell even as memory fails.
- **Workplace abuse and humiliation are morally legible** — The bar notices harms ordinary institutions minimize: abusive customers, exploitative bosses, public humiliation and people forced to swallow anger to keep a job.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Dream intervention often bypasses consent** — The team drugs, manipulates or enters dreams without fully informed permission. The show treats outcomes as benevolent more often than it interrogates coercion.
- **The quota can instrumentalize suffering** — Early Weol-ju sometimes sees customers as numbers needed for release, making her later genuine care an important correction.
- **The afterlife bureaucracy is punitive** — Harsh supernatural punishments sit uneasily beside the show's restorative ethic, though this contrast often motivates criticism of rigid judgment.
- **Comedy can soften serious harm abruptly** — The tone sometimes moves quickly between abuse/grief and slapstick, reducing the felt weight of individual cases even when the underlying compassion is strong.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x02** — Kang-bae questions whether people really want hidden worries forced into the open.
- **1x04** — An abusive customer targets a service worker and is challenged rather than allowed to humiliate them.
- **1x05** — Weol-ju cooks for Kang-bae while he is ill; he reveals no one has cooked for him at home before.
- **1x08** — A dead writer wants the person she loved to know the truth she was too ashamed to say while alive.
- **mid-series found-family arc** — The trio's concern shifts from quotas and assignments toward whether one another has eaten, slept, healed or is emotionally safe.
- **1x11** — Chief Gwi's willingness to risk himself for Weol-ju turns past responsibility into active sacrifice.
