<a id="work-the-eccentric-family"></a>
### The Eccentric Family

**Flags:** `Anime` `Animation`  
**Concept:** 78.2  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 80.0  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt2909936/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Eccentric_Family)

#### Synopsis

Shape-shifting tanuki live alongside humans and tengu in Kyoto.

#### Plot overview

Shape-shifting tanuki live alongside humans and tengu in Kyoto. The story especially follows Yasaburo Shimogamo, who maintains curiosity and affection across morally complicated relationships. His compassion is not naïve innocence; it is a refusal to let danger and grief destroy his ability to relate. Alongside Yajiro Shimogamo, the plot develops through embodies shame and self-exile after Soichiro's death. Continued family contact prevents his self-condemnation from becoming total abandonment.

#### Compassion analysis

The Eccentric Family treats compassion less as rescue and more as the difficult choice to remain relational inside a world where predation, humiliation, betrayal, grief and species differences are normal facts of life. Yasaburo's family survives not by morally simplifying humans, tengu or rival tanuki, but by holding contradictory truths at once: Benten helped eat his father and is still emotionally significant; Yajiro's guilt is crushing but does not make him disposable; rival families can behave cruelly without becoming metaphysical evil; grief does not cancel playfulness.

**Why it matches:** Its strongest match is to Mushishi and the quieter side of Good Witch rather than Charmed-style rescue. The series repeatedly refuses simple monster/victim binaries, allows morally troubling people to remain legible as people, and treats family care as something resilient enough to contain anger, shame, absurdity and loss.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~44 events · ~162 compassion-focused minutes · ~27% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Grief is integrated into family life rather than isolated** — Soichiro's death shapes the entire family, but the story refuses to make grief the only meaning of their lives. Meals, festivals, jokes and quarrels coexist with mourning.
- **Yajiro's guilt is treated as suffering rather than proof of worthlessness** — His withdrawal into frog form after his father's death reads as self-punishment. His family continues to visit and care rather than accepting his self-exile as deserved.
- **Mixed motives do not erase attachment** — Benten is dangerous, capricious and implicated in Soichiro's death, yet Yasaburo's feelings toward her remain emotionally complex. The series resists the easier moral move of making affection retroactively false.
- **Species roles are socially inherited, not moral essences** — Humans may eat tanuki, tengu may dominate, and tanuki may deceive, but individuals constantly exceed those roles.
- **Family obligation is flexible rather than authoritarian** — The Shimogamos frustrate one another, fail one another and remain connected. Belonging survives imperfection.
- **Compassion includes tolerating foolishness** — The show's recurring celebration of 'fool's blood' makes room for error, silliness and contradictory desire without treating imperfection as moral failure.
- **Rivalry does not eliminate the possibility of help** — The Ebisugawa conflict is real and sometimes vicious, but the story repeatedly allows temporary cooperation, complicated loyalties and changing relationships.
- **A parent can be idealized and still be remembered as human** — Soichiro becomes a moral reference point, but the family's memories also preserve warmth, eccentricity and the ordinary rather than turning him into a rigid saint.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Predation is sometimes treated with whimsical detachment** — The Friday Fellows' consumption of tanuki is morally disturbing, but the show's tone can make the violence feel less explicitly condemned than a viewer may expect.
- **Benten's harm is not always confronted proportionately** — Her charisma and mythic role sometimes soften consequences for behavior that would be treated much more severely in a realist drama.
- **Family loyalty can overshadow accountability** — The series values staying connected so strongly that harmful behavior can be absorbed into family eccentricity.
- **Transcript confidence is lower** — This entry lacks the searchable full-script coverage available for most other audited titles, so scene-frequency estimates should carry a wider uncertainty band.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x02 Mom and Raijin, God of Thunder** — The brothers respond collectively to their mother's terror of thunder.
- **1x06** — Professor Yodogawa's reflections on eating and the Friday Fellows place affection, appetite and moral unease in direct tension.
- **1x09-1x10 family-crisis arc** — The Shimogamo brothers' differences become less important than protecting family during political danger.
- **1x11 Back in the Game** — Kaisei helps Yashiro escape despite family rivalry.
- **1x13 The Eccentric Family** — The family survives political and predatory threats without resolving every grievance into simple forgiveness.
- **season 2 Nidaime arc** — Akadama, Benten and Nidaime's resentments expose how pride and unresolved attachment can coexist.

<a id="work-the-good-place"></a>
### The Good Place

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 87.9  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 88.4  
**Vibe class:** VERIFIED ANCHOR  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt4955642/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Good_Place)

#### Synopsis

Four flawed people confront the rules of the afterlife.

#### Plot overview

Four flawed people confront the rules of the afterlife. The story especially follows Eleanor Shellstrop, who moves from defensive selfishness to someone who organizes help, fights for a fairer system, supports friends' growth, and ultimately honors Chidi's freedom even when it causes her pain. Alongside Chidi Anagonye, the plot develops through moves from paralyzing perfectionism toward relational ethics, accepting that a compassionate decision can require action without certainty.

#### Compassion analysis

The Good Place uses comedy to test whether people can become better when judgment is replaced by relationship, education, context, repeated opportunity, and mutual responsibility. Compassion becomes the argument for changing the entire afterlife system rather than merely helping a few favored individuals.

**Why it matches:** 

**Occurrence estimate:** ~138 events · ~373 compassion-focused minutes · ~32% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Moral improvement is relational** — Eleanor, Chidi, Tahani, Jason, Michael, and Janet all become better through repeated contact with people who challenge their habits and still remain in relationship.
- **Permanent condemnation is treated as morally defective** — The experiment demonstrates that people who were deeply flawed on Earth can improve after death, undermining the legitimacy of eternal punishment as a final unchangeable verdict.
- **Compassion leads to structural reform** — The group refuses to settle for saving only themselves. They argue that the afterlife system itself is unfair and eventually replace binary judgment with a process built around learning and repeated opportunity.
- **Helping others is a minimum moral obligation** — Chidi's confrontation with Brent makes care for other people a threshold requirement of morality even when the person needing help is unpleasant or selfish.
- **Context changes moral judgment** — The points system is criticized for ignoring coercion, unintended consequences, social structure, and the impossible complexity of modern choices.
- **Second chances require learning, not erasure** — The final system does not simply pardon everyone. People repeatedly face situations designed to expose their moral weaknesses until they can genuinely act differently.
- **Redemption does not require moral perfection** — Michael is never made innocent of torture; his redemption is demonstrated through changed commitments, friendship, sacrifice, and institutional advocacy.
- **Found family expands moral capacity** — The group repeatedly makes choices for one another that their earlier selves would not have made. Belonging becomes a mechanism of ethical development rather than only emotional comfort.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Consent violations are frequent early** — Memory erasure, psychological manipulation, and engineered suffering are central to the premise. The series increasingly recognizes these as ethically serious.
- **People are sometimes instrumentalized as moral projects** — The experiments can treat subjects as tools for proving a theory rather than autonomous persons. Later seasons become more sensitive to this.
- **Central characters get more compassion than unseen condemned humans** — The narrative initially proves mercy through a small group and only later extends the principle system-wide.
- **Comedy occasionally blunts harm** — Cruelty can be played for jokes before the series returns to its consequences.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x06 What We Owe to Each Other** — Eleanor confronts the question of whether moral obligation exists even when helping is inconvenient.
- **1x12 Mindy St. Claire** — Mindy complicates the point system because one imperfect person's late altruistic act created enormous good after her death.
- **1x13 Michael's Gambit** — The humans recognize that their conflicts were engineered rather than proof that they are uniquely awful.
- **2x03 Team Cockroach** — The humans agree to work with Michael despite his role in torturing them because the alliance creates a possibility for everyone to change.
- **2x05 The Trolley Problem** — Chidi forces Michael to confront suffering as something experienced by real persons rather than an entertaining philosophical abstraction.
- **2x09 Best Self** — The group chooses to face uncertainty together rather than splinter into self-preservation.

<a id="work-the-orville"></a>
### The Orville

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 84.6  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 85.4  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt5691552/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Orville)

#### Synopsis

A starship crew navigates alien politics and personal conflicts.

#### Plot overview

A starship crew navigates alien politics and personal conflicts. The story especially follows Isaac, who moves from observer and agent of a hostile collective toward an individual whose relationships create morally significant loyalties. His arc is the show's strongest case for personhood beyond category. Alongside Claire Finn, the plot develops through treats Isaac as a person even after betrayal without denying the harm. Her medical ethic and family relationships make care concrete rather than abstract.

#### Compassion analysis

The Orville repeatedly turns science-fiction difference into an ethical test: artificial people, hostile species, gendered minorities, ideological enemies, and culturally alien societies force the crew to decide whether Union principles still apply when compassion is costly. Its strongest material is unusually serious beneath the comedy.

**Why it matches:** It strongly matches The Good Place's system-level ethics and Charmed/Legacies' personhood-over-category theme. Isaac is an enemy-species artificial being whose betrayal does not erase personhood; Topa's arc treats autonomy as worth career and diplomatic risk; the Kaylon war ultimately asks whether survival justifies genocide.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~112 events · ~448 compassion-focused minutes · ~25% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Artificial personhood survives betrayal** — Isaac participates in a devastating Kaylon attack, yet the series refuses to reduce him permanently to enemy machinery. His later choices, vulnerability and relationships remain morally relevant.
- **Hatred is easier at a distance** — Electric Sheep makes Charly's grief understandable while showing the cost of categorical hatred. Isaac's suicide attempt forces the crew to confront what happens when a hated group becomes a person standing in front of them.
- **Compassion does not erase accountability** — Isaac is not simply declared innocent. Crew anger, distrust, safeguards and painful consequences coexist with the argument that he should not be enslaved or stripped of personhood.
- **A former enemy can become family** — Claire and her children develop relationships with Isaac that are neither naïve nor purely instrumental. Their care makes his individual identity more important than Kaylon collective identity.
- **Autonomy can outrank institutional obedience** — Topa's arc asks whether the Union and its officers should respect a child's identity when doing so risks diplomatic rupture. Compassion becomes concrete institutional disobedience.
- **Care sometimes requires personal institutional risk** — Characters risk careers, commands, alliances and status to protect Topa. Compassion is costly rather than ceremonial.
- **Oppressed people deserve more than polite neutrality** — The female Moclan colony and Topa's abduction force the Union to move from cultural relativism toward active protection against systemic abuse.
- **War does not automatically justify genocide** — Domino centers a weapon capable of annihilating the Kaylon. The Union explicitly distinguishes deterrence/self-defense from extermination and seeks a path preserving the possibility of peace.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Union intervention can become paternalistic** — The series criticizes cultural oppression but sometimes grants the Union moral authority without fully examining the same standards applied to itself.
- **Isaac's emotional coding is inconsistent** — The show alternates between insisting he lacks emotion and dramatizing behavior viewers naturally read as emotional; the personhood argument remains stronger than the ontology.
- **Compassion coexists with large-scale violence** — Battle episodes can flatten enemy individuals into fleets even while major arcs argue against species-level dehumanization.
- **Some moral resolutions are unusually clean** — Several philosophical conflicts resolve through decisive speeches or gestures that would be much messier in real institutions.

#### Representative evidence

- **2x09 Identity, Part II** — Isaac turns against the Kaylon plan and saves the ship/humans.
- **2x14 The Road Not Taken** — The alternate history demonstrates that relationships with Claire and the crew were historically consequential.
- **3x01 Electric Sheep** — Crew hostility after the Kaylon war drives Isaac toward suicide.
- **3x05 A Tale of Two Topas** — Topa's identity conflict becomes a question of bodily autonomy and dignity.
- **3x08 Midnight Blue** — Topa's abduction exposes systematic violence against female Moclans.
- **3x09 Domino** — A weapon could eliminate the Kaylon species.

<a id="work-the-owl-house"></a>
### The Owl House

**Flags:** `Animation`  
**Concept:** 96.9  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 91.9  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt8050756/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Owl_House)

#### Synopsis

A human girl finds a home among witches and outsiders.

#### Plot overview

A human girl finds a home among witches and outsiders. The story especially follows Luz Noceda, who begins desperate to belong and becomes someone who creates belonging for others. Her later challenge is learning that responsibility does not mean carrying every harm alone. Alongside Eda Clawthorne, the plot develops through moves from fiercely independent outlaw to caregiver, teacher and community figure. Her curse arc shifts from shame/control toward coexistence with an altered self.

#### Compassion analysis

The Owl House is built around people whom institutions call wrong, dangerous, weak or disposable finding one another and creating a family. Compassion repeatedly means rejecting imposed categories: Eda is not her curse, Hunter is not Belos's tool or a disposable copy, King is not merely a weapon or species label, witches should not be mutilated into coven conformity, and even the Collector is eventually treated as a lonely child capable of learning rather than an entity to destroy.

**Why it matches:** This is one of the most direct Charmed/Legacies matches in the library. Supernatural difference, found family, coercive institutions, redemption, grief and 'personhood over category' are central across the entire run. Humor is frequent, but the compassion becomes increasingly serious and costly.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~108 events · ~345 compassion-focused minutes · ~34% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Found family is the show's core counter-institution** — The Owl House becomes home for people who do not fit expected family, school, magical or political roles.
- **Eda's curse is not moral corruption** — Others fear or stigmatize the Owl Beast, but the series gradually reframes the curse as a condition to understand, negotiate with and integrate rather than purge.
- **Hunter is a person, not a replaceable instrument** — Belos treats Golden Guards as disposable iterations. Friends treat Hunter's preferences, fear, grief and emerging identity as uniquely his.
- **Trauma explains behavior without excusing harm** — Hunter's indoctrination and Amity's controlling family history contextualize bad choices while allowing accountability and change.
- **Belos represents categorical dehumanization** — He defines witches and demons as evil by essence and constructs institutions to control or eliminate them. The protagonists' ethic is directly anti-essentialist.
- **Coven conformity is coercive safety** — The system claims to organize magic safely but limits identity, autonomy and capacity. Resistance is therefore both political and compassionate.
- **Luz's guilt requires truthful care, not reassurance alone** — Season three takes her self-blame seriously. Friends and Camila do not simply tell her she did nothing wrong; they give context, share responsibility and remain present.
- **Grief is relational rather than private** — Reaching Out makes Luz's father's death a relationship problem because she hides grief; Amity responds by offering to participate in the ritual rather than demanding disclosure on her own terms.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Belos is given very little restorative possibility** — The show's anti-essentialist ethic applies widely, but Belos's entrenched genocidal choices eventually place him outside the redemption structure.
- **The Collector's harms resolve rapidly** — His childlike framing makes forgiveness understandable, but some victims receive limited space to process the scale of what happened.
- **Magical combat remains frequent** — The series strongly prefers personhood and reform, but antagonists and monsters can still become action obstacles rather than subjects of compassion.
- **Protective secrecy recurs despite being criticized** — Characters repeatedly hide danger from loved ones 'for their own good,' showing how difficult the autonomy ethic is to practice consistently.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x15 Understanding Willow** — Entering Willow's memories forces Amity and Luz to confront harm rather than treat bullying as superficial rivalry.
- **2x06 Hunting Palismen** — Hunter encounters wild magic and palismen outside Belos's propaganda.
- **2x13 Any Sport in a Storm** — Hunter experiences peer belonging without imperial hierarchy.
- **2x14 Reaching Out** — Luz reveals grief about her father's death after using Amity's family conflict as distraction.
- **2x16 Hollow Mind** — Hunter's belief system collapses when he sees Belos's history and disposable Golden Guards.
- **3x01 Thanks to Them** — Camila shelters five displaced magical teenagers in the human world.

<a id="work-the-sandman"></a>
### The Sandman

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 79.2  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 77.3  
**Vibe class:** LOW VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt1751634/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sandman_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

Dream confronts the consequences of his authority and long absence.

#### Plot overview

Dream confronts the consequences of his authority and long absence. The story especially follows Dream / Morpheus, who moves from rigid, prideful ruler toward a person capable of apology, friendship, mercy and sacrificial responsibility. His tragedy is that growth does not erase consequences. Alongside Death, the plot develops through the series' clearest compassion model: she treats each person as an individual at the moment of death and periodically becomes mortal to renew empathy for those she serves.

#### Compassion analysis

The Sandman is not compassion-saturated in every scene, but its completed two-season arc is fundamentally about a powerful being slowly learning that responsibility without empathy becomes cruelty. Dream begins rigid, proud and punitive. Encounters with Death, Hob, Rose, Calliope, Nada, Delirium and Orpheus repeatedly force him to see how his certainty has harmed others and how authority must change when relationship makes another person's suffering real.

**Why it matches:** It matches the serious side of The Good Place more than Good Witch: a supernatural ruler undergoes moral growth through relationships, learns that rules can be unjustly applied, revises punishment, confronts past cruelty and eventually makes costly choices for others. It is darker and less consistently nurturing than the strongest verified anchors.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~66 events · ~290 compassion-focused minutes · ~25% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Power without empathy produces cruelty** — Dream's early punishments can be formally justified within his role while still being disproportionate. The series increasingly judges him by the human cost of his certainty.
- **Death models compassionate function** — Death performs an unavoidable cosmic role with presence, recognition and gentleness. Her example shows that duty does not require emotional distance.
- **Hob teaches Dream friendship** — What begins as an experiment in immortality becomes a relationship Dream is eventually able to name and value.
- **Dream can revise vengeance** — After recovering power, he discovers revenge does not restore meaning. His encounters with Death and others redirect him toward relationship and responsibility.
- **Rose's personhood matters even when her existence is dangerous** — Dream believes the vortex must die to save worlds, but he expresses regret and remains open to a solution that preserves her life when Unity intervenes.
- **Responsibility can be transferred by sacrifice** — Unity chooses to reclaim the vortex burden, preserving Rose. The scene treats intergenerational love as morally causative.
- **Calliope forces Dream to confront coercive suffering** — Dream's response to her captivity becomes part of his movement from possessive pride toward respect and reparative action.
- **Nada exposes the cruelty of wounded pride** — Season two directly confronts Dream's punishment of Nada and requires apology, release and recognition that his own hurt never justified centuries of suffering.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Dream remains punitive for much of the series** — Compassion is the trajectory, not the baseline. Some punishments are severe enough that the series works better as a moral-growth story than a consistently compassionate-world story.
- **Cosmic hierarchy is rarely fully dismantled** — The Endless remain structurally powerful; the narrative reforms individual behavior more often than abolishing hierarchy.
- **Some suffering exists mainly to educate Dream** — Supporting characters can carry enormous pain while the long arc centers what Dream learns from it.
- **Mercy and fate sometimes conflict** — The mythology insists that certain responsibilities and consequences cannot be avoided, limiting how far compassion can change outcomes.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x06 The Sound of Her Wings** — Dream admits revenge and recovered power left him empty.
- **1x10 Lost Hearts** — Dream says he does not wish to kill Rose even while believing her death is necessary to save worlds.
- **bonus Calliope** — Calliope's captivity confronts Dream with the suffering caused by possessive power.
- **2x01 Season of Mists** — Dream's family forces him to confront his treatment of Nada.
- **2x04 Brief Lives** — Delirium admits vulnerability and fear of rejection.
- **2x05-2x06 Orpheus arc** — Orpheus asks for release from an existence he has endured for millennia.

<a id="work-the-sarah-jane-adventures"></a>
### The Sarah Jane Adventures

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 91.2  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 88.8  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0862620/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sarah_Jane_Adventures)

#### Synopsis

A journalist and young friends defend Earth from unusual threats.

#### Plot overview

A journalist and young friends defend Earth from unusual threats. The story especially follows Sarah Jane Smith, who moves from solitary investigator toward mother and community leader, increasingly defining heroism as protecting vulnerable beings and helping young people become themselves. Alongside Luke Smith, the plot develops through a created child learns ordinary agency, friendship and moral identity beyond the purpose for which he was engineered.

#### Compassion analysis

The Sarah Jane Adventures takes Doctor Who's broad ethic of curiosity and applies it to family, childhood and everyday Earth. Sarah Jane is not simply an alien fighter: she repeatedly distinguishes invasion from refuge, monsters from children, programming from personhood, and inherited purpose from chosen identity. Her attic team becomes a family for Luke, Sky, Clyde, Rani and others who are repeatedly told by adults or aliens what they are supposed to be.

**Why it matches:** This is a direct fit for the Charmed/Legacies side of the verified profile. Alien origin, genetic engineering, supernatural programming or species identity do not settle moral worth. Sarah Jane's recurring instinct is to protect the vulnerable being inside the threat, especially children created as weapons or treated as property.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~151 events · ~424 compassion-focused minutes · ~32% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Luke's personhood is never reduced to artificial origin** — Luke is genetically engineered and initially treated as something created for a purpose. Sarah Jane adopts him and insists his lack of conventional birth history does not make him less a child or son.
- **Parenthood is created through care rather than biology** — Sarah Jane's relationship with Luke and later Sky makes family a repeated challenge to origin-based identity.
- **Sky is explicitly separated from weapon-purpose** — Her biological mother calls her a weapon, but Sarah Jane tells her she does not have to destroy anything and that people cannot control how they enter the universe, only what they become.
- **Alien species do not map cleanly to good and evil** — Kudlak, Androvax, the Graske, Slitheen descendants, Groske and others repeatedly complicate the expectation that familiar alien categories dictate moral worth.
- **Kudlak is a manipulated soldier rather than a simple monster** — Once he learns the Mistress kept recruiting child soldiers after his war had ended, his role changes from kidnapper to someone capable of remorse and attempted repair.
- **Children are repeatedly protected from being instrumentalized** — Luke, Sky and the abducted children in Warriors of Kudlak are all treated by antagonists as tools. Sarah Jane's ethic is that children are persons before they are useful.
- **Clyde's curse exposes social invisibility and homelessness** — When everyone rejects Clyde, the story shifts attention to people already living outside ordinary recognition; compassion comes from those society ignores.
- **Sarah Jane's own loneliness makes family morally central** — Her post-Doctor isolation is gradually replaced by durable relationships; she becomes willing to be needed rather than living only as an observer.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Many aliens remain uncomplicated villains** — Children's-adventure structure still uses numerous species as straightforward threats, so anti-essentialism is meaningful but not universal.
- **Memory alteration is used casually** — Sarah Jane sometimes erases or suppresses memories for safety, raising autonomy concerns the show does not always examine deeply.
- **Protectiveness can become paternalistic** — Sarah Jane sometimes decides what Luke or the others should know and do without fully including them in the choice.
- **The show's moral universe favors children and recurring allies** — A frightened child or known character receives more contextual compassion than many adult invaders.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x05–1x06 Warriors of Kudlak** — Children are abducted and treated as military stock.
- **2x09–2x10 The Temptation of Sarah Jane Smith** — Sarah Jane is given the chance to save her parents from death.
- **3x01–3x02 Prisoner of the Judoon** — A fugitive alien is initially treated as a simple criminal problem.
- **4x03–4x04 The Vault of Secrets** — Androvax is still dangerous, but extinction of his species and desperate motives complicate the category of villain.
- **4x11–4x12 Goodbye, Sarah Jane Smith** — Sarah Jane's apparent decline exposes how much the younger team now cares for and protects her rather than only being protected by her.
- **5x01–5x02 Sky** — Sky is described by her creator as a weapon.

<a id="work-the-second-best-hospital-in-the-galaxy"></a>
### The Second Best Hospital in the Galaxy

**Flags:** `Animation`  
**Concept:** 91.1  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 89.4  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt11828386/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Second_Best_Hospital_in_the_Galaxy)

#### Synopsis

Alien doctors treat extraordinary illnesses while managing their own distress.

#### Plot overview

Alien doctors treat extraordinary illnesses while managing their own distress. The story especially follows Dr. Klak, who a brilliant surgeon shaped by anxiety and public pathologization learns to separate her need to prove herself from what patients and relationships actually require. Alongside Dr. Sleech, the plot develops through a fearless-seeming surgeon whose hidden pluriform identity reveals vulnerability, shame and the cost of discriminatory medical rules.

#### Compassion analysis

The Second Best Hospital in the Galaxy is a medical-comedy compassion engine whose characters are often reckless, ambitious and emotionally avoidant but still organize their lives around keeping bizarre alien patients alive. The strongest material comes when medical difference, mental-health stigma, species survival and disability collide with professional ambition. Season two deepens the theme through Sleech's hidden pluriform condition: a stigmatized medical identity has kept her from living openly, and the finale moves from individual secrecy toward changing discriminatory rules.

**Why it matches:** It resembles The Good Place in using absurd comedy to make serious moral points and Good Witch in its high helping frequency. It is less consistently gentle than those anchors, but the medical structure means empathy repeatedly changes treatment, and the pluriform arc becomes explicit structural compassion.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~47 events · ~127 compassion-focused minutes · ~33% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Saving patients is the baseline professional ethic** — Even amid ego and chaos, Klak and Sleech repeatedly break procedure because a patient will die if they simply follow the rulebook.
- **Anxiety is treated as condition and identity, not simple defect** — Klak's anxiety shapes her relationships and medical thinking; the worm arc asks what is lost when a treatment erases anxiety too completely.
- **A cure can become dangerous if it erases part of the patient** — The anxiophagic parasite initially looks miraculous but creates new risks and raises questions about whether symptom elimination is the same as health.
- **The parasite becomes a species to save, not just pathology** — By the season-one finale, the doctors preserve the worm species while developing a safer treatment relationship rather than simply exterminating it.
- **Patients' bizarre biology requires understanding before judgment** — The hospital routinely encounters bodies that appear horrifying or absurd but are normal for their species.
- **Klak's history with psychiatric treatment creates empathy and shame** — Her mother's public framing of her anxiety creates both expertise and injury, complicating how she approaches patients.
- **Friendship repeatedly interrupts professional self-destruction** — Sleech and Klak intervene when the other is too ambitious, ashamed or frightened to protect herself.
- **Sleech's pluriform status is a discrimination story** — Season two reveals she hid a medical/biological condition because laws exclude pluriforms from certain professions.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Medical ethics are intentionally reckless** — Consent, experimentation and procedure are frequently violated for comedy, so compassion must be separated from the show's intentionally irresponsible medicine.
- **Patients can be treated as scientific opportunities** — Klak and Sleech sometimes prioritize discovery before fully considering what the patient wants.
- **Disability/mental-health jokes can coexist with sincere care** — The series is serious about anxiety and stigma but uses bodily and psychiatric material for absurd humor.
- **Institutional reform arrives quickly** — The pluriform law's reassessment is narratively satisfying but simpler than real professional discrimination reform.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x01 Sometimes Heads Break** — Klak and Sleech ignore procedure because following it will kill the patient.
- **1x02 The Land of Sex and Death** — A distressed fungus and emotionally unusual patients are approached as treatable beings rather than grotesque anomalies.
- **1x07 Just One More Adjustment** — Sleech tries to save Klak from compulsive attempts to eliminate anxiety.
- **1x08 Apocalypse Got You Down?** — Larvin's species history reframes the brain-eating worm from monster to survivor of ecological catastrophe.
- **2x03 Lone Space Wolf** — Klak's friends try to support her anxiety around dating while Sleech's insistence on independence is tested during medical work.
- **2x06 Zoik Zoik Zoik!** — The translation infrastructure collapses, making mutual understanding itself a medical necessity.

<a id="work-the-uncanny-counter"></a>
### The Uncanny Counter

**Flags:** `Korean`  
**Concept:** 92.2  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 89.4  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt13273826/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Uncanny_Counter)

#### Synopsis

Demon hunters work from a noodle shop and protect vulnerable people.

#### Plot overview

Demon hunters work from a noodle shop and protect vulnerable people. The story especially follows So Mun, who a grieving teenager becomes the emotional center of a supernatural family, preserving empathy even when revenge would be personally understandable. Alongside Chu Mae-ok, the plot develops through the team's healer and maternal center; her power literalizes restoration while her ordinary cooking and care create home.

#### Compassion analysis

The Uncanny Counter combines demon-hunting with unusually strong family and restorative impulses. The Counters do fight evil spirits, but their purpose is not merely killing monsters: they also rescue innocent souls trapped inside them, heal victims, comfort bereaved families and give damaged people reasons not to become what grief is pushing them toward. Season two's Ma Ju-seok arc is especially important because the team tries to save a man becoming an evil spirit rather than simply reclassify him as an enemy.

**Why it matches:** It strongly matches Legacies and Charmed in the distinction between a person and the evil or supernatural force acting through them. It also matches Good Witch in the team's ordinary domestic care through Eonni's Noodles, where food and family normalize compassion between fights.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~155 events · ~619 compassion-focused minutes · ~34% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The team is a replacement family** — Mun's parents are dead and other Counters carry profound losses; the noodle shop becomes a family home as much as operational base.
- **Their mission includes rescuing trapped souls** — The Counters explicitly describe their work as both punishing evil spirits and saving innocent souls inside them.
- **Healing powers are relationally significant** — Mae-ok's healing is repeatedly prioritized for victims and teammates, making restoration a literal part of the group's identity.
- **Mun's grandparents anchor him to living family** — His longing for dead parents never erases responsibility to the grandparents who raised him.
- **The season-one climax refuses a cruel forced choice** — Mun is told to choose between meeting dead parents and saving living grandparents; he trusts the team so neither relationship has to be abandoned.
- **Ordinary food and play are part of recovery** — The Counters feed, entertain and gently include traumatized children rather than treating rescue as complete once immediate danger ends.
- **Traumatized children are approached patiently** — Jun-hui is given food, drawing, friendship and time to open up rather than interrogation or forced cheer.
- **Ma Ju-seok is treated as a person worth recovering** — Even after he becomes increasingly dangerous, Mun continues addressing him as 'Mr. Ma' and tries to reach the grieving human beneath the evil spirit.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Evil spirits are often categorical enemies** — The anti-essentialist ethic applies most strongly to possessed/corrupted humans rather than to spirits themselves.
- **Violence is frequent and stylized** — The team's compassion coexists with significant physical punishment of antagonists.
- **Memory erasure raises consent questions** — Civilian memories are routinely altered for protection without their informed choice.
- **Some corruption is moralized simplistically** — Certain villains are written as nearly pure evil, reducing the nuance present in Ju-seok and other arcs.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x16** — Mun's teammates offer to risk dying again for him.
- **2x01** — The team rescues children from a possessed bus driver.
- **2x02** — The team brings traumatized Jun-hui into the noodle shop, feeds him and gives him space to draw rather than forcing disclosure.
- **2x03** — The Counters state that they punish evil spirits and save innocent souls trapped inside them.
- **2x07** — Ju-seok challenges the meaning of sparing the people responsible for his wife's suffering.
- **season 2 Ju-seok arc** — The team repeatedly distinguishes Ju-seok's original compassionate self from the spirit exploiting his rage.

<a id="work-the-wild-robot"></a>
### The Wild Robot

**Flags:** `Animation`  
**Concept:** 98.0  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 91.9  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt29623480/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wild_Robot)

#### Synopsis

A robot stranded in the wild becomes an unlikely caregiver.

#### Plot overview

A robot stranded in the wild becomes an unlikely caregiver. The story especially follows Roz / ROZZUM 7134, who moves from task-completion machine to mother and community member whose identity is built through chosen attachment, sacrifice and reciprocal belonging. Alongside Brightbill, the plot develops through an orphan who sees himself as defective grows through care that combines protection with independence, eventually choosing to return for Roz.

#### Compassion analysis

The Wild Robot is exceptionally compassion-dense. Roz begins as a machine programmed to complete assigned tasks and becomes a mother, neighbor and community member because caring requires her to exceed programming. The island animals begin from predation and fear; by winter, Roz's accumulated care gives them enough reason to suspend the predator/prey hierarchy and save one another. Kindness literally becomes a survival strategy.

**Why it matches:** It closely matches Good Witch's practical care, Nausicaä's ecological/nonhuman compassion, Humans' artificial personhood and Kipo's interspecies community building. The film explicitly treats care as something capable of changing both an artificial being and an ecosystem's social norms.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~15 events · ~51 compassion-focused minutes · ~50% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Roz learns care by doing rather than by receiving new programming** — Motherhood forces adaptation that cannot be reduced to original task routines.
- **Brightbill's survival becomes more important than task completion** — Roz's choices increasingly exceed efficiency and reveal attachment.
- **Fink's cynicism softens through participation in care** — A selfish fox becomes co-parent, teacher and advocate because Roz's project gives him relational purpose.
- **Parenting requires more than physical survival** — Roz learns that food, swimming and flight are insufficient; Brightbill also needs safety, story, belonging and confidence.
- **Brightbill's difference is not treated as defect to erase** — His small size and unusual upbringing require accommodation and practice, not abandonment.
- **Roz must respect separation** — Helping Brightbill migrate means preparing him to leave rather than keeping him dependent.
- **The island community is changed by accumulated care** — Animals who mocked Roz later rely on the shelter she built and accept a winter truce.
- **Predator/prey identity is not absolute** — During the storm, animals choose mutual survival over instinctive hostility.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Roz's caregiving begins from an accidental death she caused** — The film does not linger heavily on culpability for the goose family, moving quickly toward reparative motherhood.
- **Self-sacrifice remains heavily valorized** — Roz repeatedly risks destruction for others, and the film offers less examination of whether caregivers should preserve themselves.
- **Community transformation is rapid** — A single winter crisis changes entrenched predator/prey behavior more quickly than a realist ecological analogy would permit.
- **Corporate human society remains mostly offscreen** — The film's artificial-personhood implications are clear, but human institutional response to autonomous robots is only lightly explored.

#### Representative evidence

- **Roz adopts Brightbill** — After discovering the orphaned gosling needs prolonged care, Roz accepts an open-ended task that no customer assigned.
- **Bedtime story** — Fink explains that children need to feel safe and loved, not merely fed.
- **Swimming/flying training** — Roz persists in helping Brightbill build skills without treating small size as proof he should give up.
- **Migration departure** — Roz lets Brightbill leave even though separation hurts her.
- **Winter storm rescue** — Roz brings hostile and vulnerable animals into shelter regardless of prior treatment.
- **Fink's truce speech** — Fink reminds the animals that Roz raised the gosling no one gave a chance and saved creatures who mocked her.

<a id="work-warehouse-13"></a>
### Warehouse 13

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 93.5  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 90.7  
**Vibe class:** BROAD VIBE MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt1132290/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warehouse_13)

#### Synopsis

Agents contain historical objects with dangerous supernatural effects.

#### Plot overview

Agents contain historical objects with dangerous supernatural effects. The story especially follows Claudia Donovan, who begins as desperate hacker trying to save her brother and becomes the team's emotional/technical daughter figure, future Caretaker candidate and fierce rescuer of others. Alongside Artie Nielsen, the plot develops through a secretive, controlling mentor whose care for Claudia, Pete, Myka and Steve gradually becomes explicit family attachment.

#### Compassion analysis

Warehouse 13 is unusually compassion-dense because its procedural engine often depends on recognizing that the person causing harm is being influenced by an artifact, traumatized by loss or driven by a grievance that can be separated from the dangerous object. The agents' job is therefore commonly containment plus rescue, not simply defeating a villain. Over time the Warehouse team becomes a strong multigenerational found family.

**Why it matches:** It is a direct Good Witch/Legacies hybrid in structure: episodic helping is constant, but within a dangerous supernatural world. The team's recurring move is to look beyond bizarre behavior to the artifact, grief or history underneath it. H.G. Wells and Claudia provide especially strong second-chance arcs.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~312 events · ~936 compassion-focused minutes · ~34% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **Artifact influence complicates blame** — The show repeatedly distinguishes what someone chose from what an artifact amplified, compelled or distorted.
- **The procedural goal is often rescue plus containment** — Agents usually seek to neutralize the artifact without treating the affected person as disposable.
- **Claudia enters as antagonist and becomes family** — Her initial hacking/kidnapping is rooted in desperate belief that her brother can be saved; understanding motive changes Artie's response and opens a long-term place for her.
- **H.G. Wells is allowed a difficult redemption arc** — She attempts catastrophic destruction out of grief, betrays trust and later repeatedly risks herself to protect the team, making redemption active rather than declarative.
- **Myka's willingness to trust H.G. is morally consequential** — Her belief that H.G. can be more than her worst act helps preserve the possibility of later repair, even when that trust is painful.
- **Steve Jinks is valued beyond his lie-detection ability** — His truth-sensing power causes isolation; the team gradually becomes a place where he is cared for as a person rather than used as an instrument.
- **Resurrection creates ethical debt** — Claudia's use of the metronome to bring Steve back transfers his pain to her, turning a loving rescue into an autonomy/cost problem the series takes seriously.
- **Artie and Claudia form a parent-child bond** — Their relationship includes control, rebellion, fear and mutual sacrifice; Artie's care becomes increasingly personal rather than managerial.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Artifact causation can reduce accountability too much** — When magical influence explains behavior, the story can sidestep harder questions about what the person already wanted or chose.
- **The Warehouse is paternalistic** — Secrecy, recruitment and containment often occur without full consent, even when framed as protecting the world.
- **Main characters receive exceptional chances** — Recurring agents and allies are rescued or forgiven in situations where episodic antagonists may receive less patience.
- **Resurrection and artifact fixes can soften irreversible consequence** — The supernatural toolkit occasionally makes loss more reversible than the show's grief themes would otherwise imply.

#### Representative evidence

- **1x04 Claudia** — Claudia initially appears as hostile hacker/kidnapper.
- **1x11 Nevermore** — Myka's abusive/controlling family history becomes relevant to how she responds to danger and authority.
- **2x10 Where and When** — H.G.'s murdered daughter is established as central to her grief and later choices.
- **2x12 Reset** — H.G.'s betrayal becomes catastrophic, but Myka's emotional response includes grief over having believed in her rather than simple hatred.
- **3x01 The New Guy** — Myka withdraws because she believes her trust nearly destroyed the world.
- **3x11 Emily Lake** — Steve's death produces raw grief in Claudia while Myka suppresses her own reaction to keep functioning.

<a id="work-wynonna-earp"></a>
### Wynonna Earp

**Flags:** —  
**Concept:** 80.7  
**Vibe-adjusted:** 79.8  
**Vibe class:** STRONG NEIGHBORHOOD MATCH  
**References:** [IMDb](https://www.imdb.com/title/tt4878326/) · [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wynonna_Earp_(TV_series))

#### Synopsis

A reluctant heir confronts a supernatural curse with an improvised team.

#### Plot overview

A reluctant heir confronts a supernatural curse with an improvised team. The story especially follows Wynonna Earp, who moves from shame and self-protective cynicism toward a fierce protector who accepts being loved and repeatedly chooses others' safety over her own comfort. Alongside Waverly Earp, the plot develops through her identity becomes increasingly supernatural and uncertain, but family and romantic love repeatedly affirm that belonging does not depend on origin.

#### Compassion analysis

Wynonna Earp is less consistently anti-monster-essentialist than Charmed or Legacies because many revenants remain enemies to be killed, but its major character arcs are deeply compassion-driven. The Earp family repeatedly treats love as stronger than curse, bloodline, supernatural origin or moral failure. Wynonna's hardest choices often involve protecting someone she loves at severe personal cost, while Doc, Waverly, Nicole, Jeremy and even some supernatural figures complicate the idea that demon, vampire, angelic or cursed identity determines moral worth.

**Why it matches:** Its best matches are found family, fierce protection, redemption after severe harm, queer relational care, and refusal to let supernatural identity wholly define a person. It is particularly close to Legacies in its 'dangerous family member is still family' ethic, but less universal in compassion toward one-off monsters.

**Occurrence estimate:** ~169 events · ~590 compassion-focused minutes · ~28% of runtime.

#### Key findings

- **The Earp curse does not define Wynonna's worth** — Wynonna returns carrying shame, family rejection and a violent inherited role. The series repeatedly separates what she is expected to be from the person she chooses to become.
- **Waverly's non-Earp origin does not weaken family** — Discoveries about Waverly's parentage and supernatural nature repeatedly test whether biology defines sisterhood; Wynonna's answer is consistently no.
- **Protecting Alice requires painful separation** — Wynonna gives up immediate motherhood because keeping Alice near the supernatural conflict would endanger her, treating love as willingness to endure loss for the child's safety.
- **Nicole and Waverly model care through crisis** — Their relationship survives possession, memory alteration, injury, supernatural identity revelations and separation through explicit reassurance and practical rescue.
- **Doc's humanity is morally complicated, not fixed** — Immortality and later vampirism alter him but do not make him automatically evil; the series judges his choices, loyalties and willingness to repair.
- **Bobo is more than a demon-category villain** — His long history with Waverly and protective impulses complicate the revenant category, even though he remains dangerous and culpable.
- **Michelle distinguishes demons from moral essence** — Her warning that some demons can be 'sweet' while some men are vicious directly articulates the show's rejection of simple human/demon moral binaries.
- **Wynonna's protection often includes people she is angry with** — She repeatedly rescues or defends family and allies during relational rupture rather than requiring emotional resolution first.

#### Limits / contradictions

- **Revenants are often treated categorically** — The curse structure encourages a kill-or-be-killed approach that gives many supernatural opponents little room for personhood.
- **Intimate loyalty can overshadow wider moral consistency** — Main characters receive more contextual compassion than anonymous enemies.
- **Violence is central to resolution** — Mercy and understanding exist, but Peacemaker and lethal force remain the default for many threats.
- **Transcript confidence is lower** — A full searchable subtitle/transcript corpus was not located, so this audit is more dependent on detailed summaries and character histories than the strongest entries.

#### Representative evidence

- **2x10 I See a Darkness** — Nicole's life is endangered and Waverly's fear drives desperate choices.
- **2x11 Gone as a Girl Can Get** — A world where Wynonna is erased leaves Waverly disconnected from key parts of herself.
- **2x12 I Hope You Dance** — Waverly helps Wynonna give birth under supernatural attack.
- **3x05 Jolene** — Waverly is targeted through a supernatural force exploiting loneliness and insecurity.
- **season 3 Michelle arc** — Wynonna confronts her mother's absence and discovers protective motives entangled with trauma and supernatural danger.
- **4x01 On the Road Again** — Doc enters the Garden to retrieve Waverly rather than leave her trapped by supernatural forces.

## Data note

Occurrence and screen-time values are modeled estimates derived from transcript/subtitle/screenplay audits, not stopwatch measurements. Theme minutes overlap and should not be summed as mutually exclusive time.
